thing, though, dear. 1 asked him to dinner. Oh, don't raise your eyebrows quite so high, love! I meant awfully well, I assure you. It struck me what a good opportunity it would be of effecting a reconciliation. For, after all, the poor fellow didn't do anything so dreadfsd And ndw that his father and your husband are both gone, I thought--" "What did you think, I wonder, dear?" "Well, I'm not quite sure now what I did think; but it was something quite kind and sensible. He asked after you so sweetly, and Seemed so anxious to hear all about you, when I said you were stopping at my house. It wazn't as if you'd had a personal quarrel in the old days, you know; and his wife's dead. And I can't deny that I had rather a weakness for him myself once, before anyone dreamt what an idiot he would be. Besides, his daughter is with him. She's seventeen, it seems, though I could hardly realise it; so he is quite the respectable family man. I fancied you ...light even hsv a. curiosity to see them both; so, .4 the spur of the moment, I said: 'Come to dinner and bring your daughter. Your sister-in- w .and her son and daughter will be with me— nobody else.' The instant I had done it I repented, and thought how more than shocking of me not to have given you the chance to choose. Will you forgive me? You can have a neadache and your dinner sent up, if you like; or I'll even wire him that you are ill, and—" "Didn't you say that you hadn't his address?" "Oh, dear, yes! No more I have. How like me What shall we do?"

"Make the best of it," replied Lady Carstairs, quietly, though her eyes were brighter than their wont. "I shall certainly not keep my room to avoid Lancelot Wade. it is he who should shrink from meeting the widow of his only brother, whom he disgraced. As for me, I have no feeling whatever in the matter. I should scarcely say he would prove a congenial dinner-table companion; but that is for you to decide, and you have decided. As, unfortunately, we have no longer husbande to watch over and protect us, we can choose for our- selves whom we shall meet; but if you will pardon me, dear, I am not sure that it will be best for Gladys to dine with us to-night." Good-natured Taady Northmuir looked distressed: "Oh, do let her, Virginia! It will be such an insult to the poor things if you ostentatiously keep your daughter out of their way—as much as to say that their very presence might contaminate her innocence. I mentioned to poor Lance that Gladys would be here as a special incentive for bringing "iis girl. I ' forget her name, though he may have told me it. And as for Ralph, it is not very likely that the fascinations of a seventeen-year-old Miss Wade, brought up anyhow on the Continent, will appeal dangerously to his fastidious taste." • "I have no fear of that ! " responded Lady Carstairs' rather haughtily. She did not wish to offend Lady Northmuir, who was Ralph's god- mother, and might leave him a good deal- of money if she chose, being rich and childless; but it was very difficult not to betray her irritation. Lady Northmuir had the grace to blush a warm, middle-aged blush, for it occurred to her that, with the best intentions in the world, she was something of a hypocrite. Had she not said impulsively to poor Lancelot Wade: "Who knows, if your daughter is a pretty girl, Carstairs may not take a fancy to her? He's celebrated for his eye for beauty.' And though they're cousins, it would be a charming little romance for her to become Lady Carstairs." Had she not said this? And now, was she not expressing almost diametrically. opposite sentiments to Virginia? But, then, there was no accounting for words, and it was so hard not to let oneself be guided by them sometimes. "Of course you've no fear, dearest," she hastened to exclaim. "And as for Gladys, she's two or three years older than this wretched child; she can take no harm. She's a will of her own, you know, too, and I wonder what she'll say if she's kept in her room like a schoolgirl on some paltry excuse, when Jack Churchill dines with us ?"

Tliis was a trump card, and Lady Northmuir knew it, though she had recklessly waited to play it till the last moment.

"Oh, is Mr. Churchill coming, too ? " Lady Carstairs said, with affected carelessness.

"Yes. I forgot to tell you that I asked him— quite informally, of course—to Meet no one but ourselves. I hadn't seen Lancelot then."

"There's no reason why I should change my plans for Gladys simply because Mr. Churchill happens to be coming," purred Lady Carstairs, with commendable fortitude, considering that the Mr. Churchill in question was "the" man of the moment, from a matrimonial point of view. "Still, dear, I don't want to be disagreeable, and, as you make a point of her appearing, I won't raise any more objections. Only pray let her sit as far as possible from—er—from her uncle and cousin (if one must call them so) at the table." _

"That's settled, then!" exclaimed Lady Northmuir, with a sigh of relief. "It's a weight off my mind, I assure you." Possibly the "weight" would have remained if she could have foreseen the events which hid them- selves below the horizon of the future!

CHAPTER 111.

THE DINNER IN BERKELEY-SQUARE.

Sybil and her father indulged in the (to her) unwonted luxury of a calito Berkeley-square. They were late, because Lancelot Wade had been interested in his novel, and put off dressing until the last moment; so that everybody was in the drawing-room, and the old butler was waiting to announce dinner, when they arrived. The girl had felt very brave, with a courage which had come with the new dress; but when the door of Lady Northmuir's house was opened by a tall footman in livery, and her eyes were dazzled with the lights in a square-pillared hall, her heart sank. Never in her seventeen years of life had she seen anything so grand as this, and she hardly knew how to face it. She went meekly upstairs to remove the golf-cape, which looked more hideous than it had ever looked in this stately house, where every- thing was so exquisitely in keeping. The French maid who was in readiness to help the young lady remove her wrap, had never divested one of Lady Northmuir's guests of so incongruous a garment as a golf-cape. And, though she was decorously civil in manner, Sybil Wade's sensitive instinct revealed to her the masked contempt of the servant.

The girl did not even glance in the long mirror before going downstairs. The sound of her name with her father's, announced by one of the footmen, made her start and colour as they entered the drawing-room together, for the custom was new to her and alarming, seeming to draw all eyes upon -er and the poor little ready-made muslin, which had appeared so desirable only an hour ago. Lancelot Wade was as completely at his ease as if there had been no gap of years between him and the days when he had been a welcome guest in such drawing-rooms as this; but Sybil, who had no memories of past grandeur, who had spent nearly all her chequered existence in shabby lodgings or third-rate pensions abroad, was stricken with the misery of acute self-consciousness for the first time in her life.

. i Ji•

With one glance, even before she had been kindly shaken hands with by her hostess, she had taken in the picture made by the beautiful room and its occupants, who appeared at first sight to be twicl their actual number.

J

Seen in contrast to Lady Northmuir's sequin& black net, Lady Carstairs's silver-embroiderek grey satin, and her daughter Gladys's wonderfu, cloudy white chiffon, poor Sybil's cheap muslin became at once a common little rag, fit only for the holiday frock of a servant. Her three-and-sixpenny slippers, her lisle thread stockings, and the gloves, which had already begun to split down the sides•of the fingers, were things fit only to be laughed at. She was dumb, unable to answer except by monosyllables the coldly courteous greetings of Lady Carstairs and Gladys Wade, who contrived, despite their civility, to let her see that she was miles removed from them; that, though the same blood ran in their veins, the daughter of Patty the little provincial music-hall singer, was not one of them, and never, never could be.

sybil was really the one too many among Lady frorthinuir's guests. As she and her father had ..en invited at the last moment to an informal di finer, hardly more than a family affair, it had been too late to ask anyone else. Lancelbt Wade took his hostess into the dining-room, and Jack Churchill—one of the richest and best-looking young men in England—had been asked especially for Gladys. Ralph—now Lord Carstairs—vms a captain in the Artillery, stationed at Aldershot, and had come up to London for a night because he particularly wished to talk over some business affairs with his mother. Therefore, he went in to dinner with her, that they might have the chance of a few extra words together, since lie was leaving town by the last train. No one was left for Sybil. But Lady Northmuir thought, consoling herself for the lack of symmetry at her table, the girl was too young to feel out of it for that reason; and au she seemed very shy, would probably rejoice at not being called upon for constant conversation.

LADIES' LETTER.

KY DEAR Nraxi a,

Is it too early to write about furs? Surely not, even though they should go hand in hand with tnuslins. Evenings are chilly, and any morning we may awake to find a touch of frost in the air. It is said that white broadtail will be the fur of the wealthy woman's hour; it is as pure as a snow- flake, and almost as perishable. Chinchilla, also a very delicate pelt, has been sought far and wide by the furriers, and I have seen a cloth coat trimmed with a generously large collar of that fur and deep turn-over cuffs. The coat was drawn with a little fulness over the figure to the left side, where it was fastened with a large silver-gilt button, a series of smaller ones in graduating sizes decora- ting the front of the skirt.

Many of the most fashionable milliners are creating large hats, a cross between a hat and a bonnet, which, with their bent brims, nodding plumes, and lace or silk embroidered draperies overshadowing the face and hair, sometimes accom- panied by strings, resemble the large poke bonnets which our grandmothers wore, happy in the feeling that they had on their heads an article that was indeed a bonnet for all the world to wonder at. .1 am convinced that black velvet hats trimmed with ostrich feathers will be very much worn-this winter, a long buckle (of cut steel, perhaps) in .some cases curving right across the front, while -the feathers curl forward from the back. Or, again, a very picturesque shape is very slightly uplifted at the left side to show a long ostrich leather resting on the hair, another curving round the crown in the opposite direction. Flat shapes predominate, and the trimming is arranged to accentuate this effect.

__Leeks and plaids are extremely fashionable in all kinds of woollen materials, and, in theless con- spicuous patterns, are eminently suitable for children's dresses. A very nice little frock for a girl between seven and 12 years old, for instance, is of checked tweed in shades of green with lines of deep crimson running through the plaiding. The skirt is plain save for three or four small tucks, and the full bodice has a large square collar forming short revers in front, of deep green glace silk edged with a narrow ecru lace applique, laid on the silk. The vest is of ecru lace, but is removable and can be replaced by a taked one of crimson Snrah matching the red line in the material. The belt is of folded green glace, and he sleeves are finished at the wrist with small the cuffs corresponding with the collar.

There is no doubt that walking skirts, quite short, will be worn this winter by those who make it a practice to take a constitutional every day. Even fashionable skirts are rather shorter than they were a couple of month 4 ago, but there will be a considerable distinction between ordinary house or driving length and that of costumes intended for wilking in in all weathers. This is as it should be for those who can afford separate suits. But for the many girls and women who have to put on in the morning the dress they-have to wear all day there can be no question that the skirt should be rather short.

The blouse-and-skirt combination has, of course, been immensely popular of late years for indoor wear, but it is becoming less fashionable, and for elder ladies, at any rate, a complete dress of some soft-failing material is certainly preferable. A very pretty house-gown can be made of pale chest- nut brown cashmere. The slightly-trained skirt has two box- 'eats at either side of the front breadth ; the ice lining is close-fitting, and on it is mounted the deep yoke of ecru lace laid over orange-red glace silk, the cashmere bodice itself being pouched in front and finished round the yoke with a ruche of brown satin ribbon. The sleeves are slightly bell-shaped with ruchings of ribbon at the cuffs. The style would look equs.lly pretty carried out in grey cashmere with black lace over rose colour silk for the yoke. Somewhat similar in type is a dress of plum-coloured cash- mere made with a skirt that has a row of small fancy steel buttons down each side pleat, and a full bodice fastenivg with a double row of similar buttons and turned back with double revers—one set of plum-coloured velvet and one of stitched vaslimere—from a yoke of cream lace over white satin, which is finished .at the neat with an un- lined lace band.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAN, OCTOBER 5 1900

CO/W • THE HOUSE BY THE RIVER.

BY MRS. C. N. WILLIAMSON, Author of "The Secret of the Pea;•ls," "The House by the Lock." "Qt w,..etheart," "The Emnstortners," "Mr Royal. 11;ghness,"

CHAPTER, I. " NOT cyr:l ;;Jr:Ls."

A. girl stood ;ooi;in,f, ci,,wr. !font a high window upon a dnii street in It was late October, and !ho a!i \Sas'it with a raw, yellow fog. A roar d r/X;,:t, mon ,!nousa fli9 booming of the 'on, came ; and the fog and the sounds seelheci 1.4, bring home to Sybil Wade a realisation of her sadness and isolation,

On the opposite side cl the street two girls of nearly her own age stopptd to say good-bye at the doorstep of a house where ens of them lived, In a window sat a middle-age,l woman, who nodded and smiled. Presently she opened the door, and one of the girls ran in, rer,Piving a kiss and a pat on the shoulder as the door closed and stint the two from Sybil's sight. "That is her mother, I suppose," thought the girl who looked down from the high window. She was always looking on at other people's home-comings and welcomings, and catching glimpses—from the outside—of their happiness. There was never anything of her own—except her father, of course ; but that was different. Sybil Wade's father was not like other girls' fathers, and Sybil Wade's life was not like other girls' lives— at least, not like what she imagined them to be. She and her father never stayed anywhere long enough for her to make friends, even if she could have cared for the sort of people with whom they Caine in contact.

Her fancy followed the girl who had gone into the house across the "way. There was a ruddy flicker of firelight on the windows, for the autumn evening was drawing in. sand the girl without a home pictured the girl who had a home, talking confidentially to her mother, telling her everything that had happened during the day, perhaps, over a cup of tea. How deliciously fragrant and fresh the tea would be, how thin the bread and butter, how crisp the cakes which the ck made especially with a view of pleasing the schoolgirl's tastes Sybil Wade was hungry, and so she thought wistfully of the tea and little cakes across the way. Her fourth-floor room did not boast the luxury of a bell; but in the passage outside there was one, only a little broken, and if she had rung it, requesting tea, the hard-worked maid would presently have come stumbling up the five flights of stairs from the basement, with a serviette-covered tray defaced by much spilt milk and hot water. But Sybil would have starved rather than make such a request, knowing, as she did know, that there would not be enough money to pay the land- lady's bill at the end of the week. She had eaten very sparingly since she and her father had come to the house in Mitchell-street, so that the "reckoning" (as she had learnt to think of it in her years of wandering through Germany and Austria) might amount to as little as possible. When Sybil's father was out, as he usually was, she said she "did not care for anything, thank you," when Mrs. Goman asked her what she would have for lunch; and that she "seldom drank tea in the afternoon."

Now, therefore, as she thought of the kind, motherly face in the opposite , house, and the welcome given the girl, and the fragrant tea and cakes of her imagination, tears rose slowly to Sybil's eyes She was hardly conscious that they were there, and did not even think of brushing them away, until a quick tap on the door made her start and hastily snatch from her pocket a much-mended handkerchief, which might have cost sixpence when it was new.

Without waiting for an answer, someone on the other side had opened the door and stepped in. "I say, Syb, I've got news for you " exclaimed a handsome, well-dressed man, who looked thirty, and would never see forty again. The girl seemed to shrink; and though she had been pale before, she was paler now. 'ls it anythine: very dreadful ? " she faltered. "Dreadful? Why, what a little grizzler you are Your mind invariably flies to the most doleful con- clusions. I wonder where you get that tendency? Certainly not from your poor mother, and not from me, for you know that I always expect the best rather than the worst."

Sybil did not answer. Perhaps if thi3 handsome, debonair man had sometimes prepared for the "worst" instead of the "best," she need not have been ready to brace herself against disaster, which seldom failed to come.

"Instead of 'something dreadful,' it's something very good," went on her father. "Look here, Syb, what have you got to wear that would do for a smart dinner this evening ? " "A smart dinner ? " The girl echoed his words as if she had scarcely been able to believe her ears, "Why, father, you are joking, of course ? " "Not a bit of it, my dear. I met an old friend to-day, Lady Northinuir, and she asked me to dine with her in Berkeley-square to-night. As an after- thought, she was kind enough to include you. Hadn't realised that I could have a grown-up daughter at first, you know. I hope you've got something decent to put on?" -

Sybil's-eyes were 'very large and distressed. "I can't possibly go, father. Indeed, I have nothing to wear—nothing that would not make you very much ashamed of me. Besides—" "Besides what ? " The handsome face was not so debonair or smiling now. The brows were drawn together, and there was a sullen look about the full red lips. The girl knew that look too well, and it brought a flame to her thin cheeks. She cast down her lashes wonderful, shadowing lashes, almost enough to make a woman's face beautiful if every other feature had been plain—and answered, with painful shyness: "I am not fit to go to such a dinner at such a house, father. I never have been. I shouldn't know what to do or what to say. I should be so miserable, and I should mortify myself and you, too."

Lancelot Wade said something under his breath that sounded like an oath.

"You are going," he remarked, aloud, "and the sooner you make up your mind to that, roy girl, the better it will be for both of us. It is now " he pulled out a common gun-metal watch—"after five o'clock, and Lady Northmuir dines at eight. If you have any alterations to make in a gown, you had better set about it at once. It seems to me I remember a white silk you had." "I haven't it now, father." Sybil did not add that it had gone, with a few other small belongings of her own, to pacify an angry landlady in Brussels a few weeks before; but she might have done so if it had not become second nature with her to spare this man's feelings when she could. "I've only the dress I have on and a cotton blouse or two," she finished; "so you see—" "I do see," grumbled Lancelot Wade. He replaced the watch in his pocket, and, forming his lips into a whistle, though they emitted no sound, he walked abruptly from the room. Something in his manner alarmed Sybil. There had been a set purpose in his eyes which she could not read. His words might have led her to believe that she had brought him to accept her view of the situation, and that he had gone away, vexed indeed, yet intending to let her remain at home without further protest. But the girl had not been motherless, travelling about the Continent with her father for ten years (since she had been a child of seven), without knowing his every mood and change of countenance. Now, she could not see how he meant to bring it about, but she was uncomfortably sure that his intention was as fixed as before. If she had dared, she would have run out into the street, to wander aimlessly about until it should be far too late to"go to the dreaded dinner; but Sybil was afraid of her father who, despite the debonair manner that won him friends, could be cruel if he were thwarted.

"No, I wouldn't dare do it," she told herself hopelessly. "There would only be something worse to bear in 'the end. And perhaps, after all, he may have given up my going." Her father's room was on the floor below, larger and more comfortable than hers. She wondered if ho had gone there; and if she had not feared to remind him of her existence at this crisis, she would have stolen down to put out his evening things—always in good repair, no matter what was the state of her wardrobe. The walls and floors were thin at this house in Mitchell-street, and sometimes Sybil could hear her father whistling or singing in his room; but there was no sound now. Fifteen minutes passed, half-an-hour ; all was silent still. But just as a church clock not far away tolled out six strokes, there were quick steps on the stairs, and the door opened without a pre- monitory knock.

•*.tiere you are. _Now make yourself as pretty as possible," ejaculated her father, dinging a large box tied up in brown paper down on the inkstained cover of the table by the windoW.

Sybil sprang op whit a lone cry of dismay. "Oh, father, you haven't buitg/it anything?" Ile laughed, though not quite good-naturedly. "You shall see," he said. And, instead of leaving the parcel for her to open, he cut the strings and tore off the wrappings himself. Lifting the cover of the tar:, and pushing aside a layer of tissue-paper, he ditpiayed a ready-made gown of white muslin, a pair of tiny white satin shoes of cheap quality, a small roll, which might mean clocked lisle-thread stockings, and an envelope, evidently containing gloves. "The best I could do at zcis end of Oxford- street," Lancelot 'Wads announced. "And I said I would bring everything home myself, as I was in a hurry. Hope they'li lit,. Why don't you thank me for taking such a lot of trouble, and making errand-boy of myself. Syb, instead of staring at m:3 as if I'd committed an nnpardonable sin ?" "I —I'M sure von meant to he kind," stammered the girl ; "but the money ? Yoit promised you would save all you could to give the landlady on Saturday." _ _ _

"Pooh r" exclaimed Wade. "Who cares for Saturday or the landlady ether? And what's the good of having a handle to one's name if one isn't to get a little credit now and then ? I'm sure that's about all mine has ever done for me; but it has done that—l will say so much for it. Don't you suppose this Mrs. Smith, or Jones, or whatever the female calls, herself, is only too delighted to have the Honourable Lancelot Wade and his daughter lodging in her fifth-rate old tenement, whether she gets paid at the end of the week or not? By Jove! She ought to pay me for stopping here. I wager it's brought-a lot of custom to the house if she's played her cards well. Now, put on your new frock, and let me hear no more or this nonsense about money, money. I'm sick to death of the word!" He gave Sybil no time to answer, but flung out of the room, shimming the door behind him. For a few moments she stood quite still, looking down at the fluffy mass of muslin in the box, with- out unfolding it; but at last, with a great sigh, she laid the shoos and gloves and stockings on the bed and took out the gown. It was a cheap, little, ready -made garment of muslin, over the flimsiest of silk; but it had ar air of fresh prettiness, with its frills and its long sash. Sybil would thankfully have taken it back to the shop where it had been bought, and asked for the money, which she regarded as belonging to the landlady, rather than to her father or herself; but she knew that this would be impossible. She would have to wear it. She would have to go to the dinner in Berkeley-square. Perhaps it would not be so very wrong, therefore, to admire the frock a little, since she must accept it as hers.

Only once before in her life had Sybil Wade possessed "-an evening dress." This had been the white silk with which she had lately parted, originally bought second-hand that she might be seen suitably clad by the officers who came to their lodgings in Vienna to play cards with her father. But that had been two years ago, and the dress had been let down twice and turned once before being transferred to the Brussels' landlady. Some of the card-playing young men had thought it good sport to flirt with "lance Wade's daughter," and Sybil had resented it,. and kept out of their way, refusing to appear when her father had visitors ; so that she had done very well without an evening gown. But this new muslin was beautiful in her eyes. "Perhaps," she said to herself, "I ought to have thanked father better. lie is careless of money, and forgets his promises sometimes; but I think he really has a kind heart, when things don't go too badly to let him remember it. I do hope I am not bard and ungrateful, and a 'prig,' as he calls me so often. I'd hate to be a prig 1" She lighted the one candle which was provided to afford illumination for the room, and, taking off the shabby black frock she wore (her only one, as she had informed her father), she let down her hair, shivering a little in the fireless room. Sybil's hair was very beautiful, and many a woman would have given thousands of pounds in exchange for it, could it have been magically trans- ferred from the head of this insignificant child. It fell, as she sat in a chair before the small cracked mirror on her dressing-table, like a veil of gold behind her slim, girlish waist, half-way down to the floor. Usually, when she had' brushed out the shining yellow waves, Sybil put them out of the way as quickly as possible, in a great coil at the nape of her neck; but to-night she took rather more pains tjuin customary tlilat her father might be pleased, and consider it an atonement, if she really had been ungrateful and hurt his sensitive feelings. The little feet, with their graceful, arched insteps, were worthy of fine silk stockings; but they looked very neat in lisle thread, and the slippers, which had the ticket with price (three shillings and sixpence) still attached to them. Sybil hardly knew herself in the dress. Her heart beat a little faster as she gazed half shyly at her own image reflected in the greenish glass. For she was only seventeen, and a very human girl, with all a girl's innocent longings and vanities under the sadness. and anxiety that tried to keep them down. She felt almost like other girls now. The dreaded dinner did not loom as formidably before her.

"I hope I shall know how to behave. I hope I sha'n't do anything very wrong at the table," she thought, anxiously, though not without hope that all might be well. She knew so little about the "proper way" of doing anything, for she had no mother to tell her, and Lancelot Wade had not been one to trouble himself over trifles, though it had suited his book to keep the child with him on his travels, posing as a loving father, and exciting the sympathy of kind-hearted landladies. "I hope shall behave like other English girls," she said again, as she went down to her father's room at last, to tell him that she was ready. He had been smoking and reading a French novel, for it would not be time to start yet for more than half-an-hour, and he could dress quickly when he set his mind to it.

"You'll do very well," he pronounced, after a long, critical look at the girl. "By Jove I shouldn't wonder if, in a few years, you'd blossom out into something really worth looking at. And, anyhow, you are like the women of my family; there's nothing of your mother in you." He spoke the last words almost viciously, for he had been a rash fool in his youth, and had thrown over brilliant prospects for a woman's sake. For that he had hated the woman when he had come to his senses again after marriage, and sometimes hz, had come near to hating little Sybil, too; but to-night he dwelt upon an idea which Lady Nortlimuir had carelessly put into his head, and felt something like interest in the girl. "She is pretty, and she might do it," he said to himself, as Sybil went away to put on the well- worn old golf-cape, which for years had been her one wrap. "Yes, she might do it. What a stroke of luck if she did!"

CHAPTER 11. LADY NORTIIMUIIe fil CONFESSION.

"My dear, I do hope I haven't done something dreadful 1" Lady Northmuir exclaimed. "Really, I feel so guilty! You know what a creature of impulse I am, always getting myself into scrapes that I'd give something to get out of. Positively, I'm afraid to tell you what I've been at this time. Her guest looked up from a book she had been readinc, and laughed. Lady Northmuir had brought into the dainty little chintz boudoir a crisp fresh- ness of atmosphere from out of doors. She was not a young woman, but she looked healthy and handsome after her exercise, for she had been walking, and in her prosperous middle age might well have posed as a modern goddess of October. "What is your latest sin ? " inquired Lady Carstairs, who appeared more than ever like a delicate, faded sketch in pastels beside her more brilliant friend. Lady Northmuir sat down, ane drawing off her gloves, held up a pair of plurals white hands to the blaze of a fragrant wood-fire.

"Well, to begin with, whom-do you suppose I met in Bond-street an hour ago, Virginia ? " Lady Carstairs shook her head. She professed herself not good at guessing. "Your black sheep brother-in-law, Lancelot Wade."

"Really ? " drawled the other, with a pretence of indifference, contradicted by a pink flush that mounted to the roots of the drab, once golden- brown, hair. "How curious that after-7-er—after everything, he should have cared to turn up in England again I haven't heard or thought of him these half-dozen years; but, if I had, I should have fancied him leading a hand-to-mouth existence abroad, making just enough out of his card-playing to keep him alive." "He looked very much alive to-day," returned Lady Northmuir, "and as handsome as ever." "Really ? " remarked Lady Carstairs again. No other word could so thoroughly have expressed her polite distaste for the subject. "Yes. He's been in town for a month, so he said; but he didn't say where be was living, gind I didn't ask him. I did ask him one

So it was arranged, and Sybil, much crushed by the supercilious eye of the footman who placed her chair, subsided into her seat in large-eyed silence. Lady Northmuir was gaily talking of Vienna with the man she had been infatuated with long ago, and Mr. Churchill was answering some questions- about his shooting-box in Scotland from Gladys. The little alien was free to observe the assembled representatives of a ivorld that had Once been her father ' s.

Looking up, she met the eyes of her cousin/ Lord Carstairs, which were fixed intently upon her face. They were pale eyes, like his mother's— almost startlingly startlingly pale in contrast to his dark skin and hair, black and straight as an Indian's. When people had- got used to this strikinc, effect, they generally pronounced Captain_ Lord Carstairs a handsome man; but Sybil did not think him so. She knew nothing of physiognomy, yet it seemed to her that the wide, deeply-curved nostrils looked cruel, and that there were hard, unscrupulous lines already graven about the mouth, slightly shaded by _a small black moustache. _ . . . _

-She turned away from the light eyes that were quietly appraising her claims to attention as if they had been the " points " of a horse he might be induced to buy, and, in her haste, awkwardly knocked against the arm of her next-door neighbour, Mr. Churchill.

"Oh, I beg your pardon ! " she exclaimed, con" fusedly. He smiled reassuringly at her, and said some- thing pleasant, which gave her an odd sense of protection, though she scarcely heard the words. Jack Churchill had hardly had a chance of observing the girl before, for Gladys Wade had claimed all his attention; but now that young woman's brother was speaking to her across the table.

Jack was what is popularly called a, "self-made* man, for though he was young, still under thirty', he owed his place in the world entirely to his own luck and courage and exertions. His father had been a poor country parson, who had died, leaving him with nothing, while he was still a boy at one of the lesser public schools. Then Jack had tried all sorts of things to make a living, not c,ariq much so long as they were honest. At last he haa gone to the States, spent his few savings in buying land, "struck oil," and made a great fortune. Ile had come back home to enjoy and spend it, merely' returning to the other side from time to time as occasion arose; but he had been in England for scarcely two years, and was only vaguely acquainted with the story of Lancelot Wade. Since he had made Gladys Wade's acquaintance he had heard a little gossip about the past of her family, and knew that there had been a "black sheep," who had been banished from the fold. Ile had heard that old Lord Carstairs, the grandfather of the present wearer of the title, had had twe sons, one of whom, the elder, he had disliked, vowing that his private fortune, which was verr, large, having descended to him from the "distaff side, should all go to the younger, Lancelot, leaving the title and estates practically barren when they came to the heir. But Lancelot had fallen in love with a prettY, girl who sang and •danced in a music-hall, and, though his father had threatened disinheritance, had persisted in his infatuation, feeling sure that he would be forgiven in the end. He never had been forgiven, however; and, cut by his family, and coldly looked upon by most of his old friends, be had left England, after being accused of cheating at cards in some Obscure garrison town where be had drifted. Old Lord Carstairs had die' keeping his word, and all the money had gone wit". the title; so that his grandson was among the fortunate ones of the earth, instead of an inarer cunious young nobleman, unable to keep up n!s position, as he would have been had the old man's will been different. Whether or no the Honourable Lancelot ha a become the dishonourable by really cheating at cards Jack Churchill did not know, and did not much care. He only knew that the small, white face of the girl looking up at him stirred some hitherto untouched chord in his heart. Jack rather thought that Ifle was in love with Gladys Wade, who was considered a beauty, ana had been much in demand since her presentation a year ago: but the haughty, self-assured r.nlll woman was not of the type which roused a 03,`•:" desire to play the knight-errant, and clo perisawm''' • chivalrous in her service. "What a lily maid it is!" he said to hiraself, he smiled at Lancelot Wade's daughter. She had marvellous eyes, he thought, strengthell sad, strangely appealing. He wondered what' child's life had been to give her such a 104 that. Hardly knowing why, except that she seer° such a piteous little waif beside the exquisitely-dressed women, he was more sorrl for her than he ever remembered feeling for ear"' and was conscious of an intense wish to do or S? something to make those great, sorrowful eyes higeu up with happiness. Just as he was so thinking, striving after faT" inspiration, there mine a sudden diversion. f` footman entered with a telegram, which he resPeev. fully banded, on a silver tray, to Carstairs. ,"Sent on from your club, my lord," the nISII murmured. Carstairs glanced at Lady Northmuir for Per" mission, then tore open the envelop. For a moment his pale grey eyes were fastened ea the paper inside, an unaccustomed tide of ceiellf slowly rising over his dark face. And then Churchill saw that a quick glance fell, first 0,11 Lancelot Wade, then Lancelot's daughter. .11 was a strange glance—a glance that Jack Church'''. was at a loss to understand.

I.—Kynoch's. CORDITE has been from the first a political explosive It was on a motion moved by Mr. Brodrick cmsuzing Sir H Campbell-Bannernan for allowing its reserve of cordite ammunition to run low that Lord Rosebery's Government fell. Mr. Chamberlain, on that occasion, openly denounced the Liberals for not placing sufficiently large orders with Kynoch's. His question was very ex- plicit: His constituency was IN SOME MEASURE IN- TERESTED in this cordite question, as the firm of Kvnoch and Company had tbeir headquarters in a suburb of Birmine ham. Perhaps the Financial Secre- tary to the War Office would say to what extent orders for cordite had been given to Messrs Kynoch. His naive admissions went even further: He had the LEGITIMATE INTEREST in the matter which, as representing his constituents, he might be expected to have. Mr. Chamberlain's "legitimate interest "in Kynoch's, however, was even closer than he eared to state. His own brother, Mr. Arthur Chamberlain. is the chairman of the company- The latest list filed at Som,Tset House on June 30, 1900, records no less than 7,296 shares in Kynoch's as in possession of nine members of the Cham- berlain family (names Chamberlain and Nttcleford). These shares at present market prices are worth some £144,171. Mr Chamberlain, in shore, bad not only a " legitimate" but a substantial family interest in Kynoch's. The" constituents" of whose welfare he was so solicitous_ were his own nearest relatives.

Kynoch's under the Unionists.

To place large orders for cordite with fivrioch's waN, therefore, a cardinal part of Unionist poliay. The matter was investigated by the Parliamentary Committee which irquired into the Army contract scandals. Kynoch's was a new firm, without experience and with limited facilities for making cordite. It was, however, the Guvernment's policy to roster Kynoch's. As Colonel Pease, the storekeeper of Naval Ordnance. put it in his evidence on July 14, they were "built up by the Government." One has to remember that the disposal of Naval Contracts is in the department of Mr Austen Chamberlain, the son of his father, and the nepiiew of the chairman of Kynoch's ; at the War:Office it depends on Mr Powell Williams,who also has "co .stituents " in Birmingham, and is proud to pose as the faithful satel- lite of Mr Chamberlain. . A Favoured Firm. w__

—The facts which came beture the Select Committee may be briefly summarised : (1) Kyncch's prices were high. Thus in January of this year seven firms tendered for the supply of

rordtte-to the Navy; the prices of other firms were 25., 2 id., and 2s. 3d. per pound ; Kynoch's sug- geFted 24. 61., 2e. 9d., or 3. (2) On that occasion—it was not the only instance of a similar favour—Mr. Powell Williams allowed Mr. Arthur Chamberlain to alter his tender. This was

done privately, and none of the other firms were al: lowed the same privilege. Mr. Powell Williams then recommended that a large order be given to Synoch's at 24. 31. per pound. (3) Kynoch's merits certainly did not warrant this singular favour. They were. as Lieutenant-Colonel Hadden, Chief Inspector of Naval Stores, stated that Kynock's were "core behindhand than other firms." As to quality, the percentage of thFir cordite rejected as unfit for use was higher than that of any other competing firm—actually reaching 46 per cent. in recent contracts.

(4) Perhaps the most damning part of the whole exposure was the evidence which showed that Kynocles.contrary to all Government rules bought the gun-cotton required in the manufacture of cordite in Germany, and even arranged with Nobels to supply them with cordite paste, the raw material of the explosive. Kynoch's cordite, in short was the worst, the dearest and the most unpunctually delivered, while to a large extent they were mere middlemen.

A Fatuous Excuse. fine official excuse for the siugutar favour shown to this firm was more than usually fatuous. Kynoch's had a factory at Arklow, in Ireland—labour is cheap in Ireland. Mr. Powell Williams accordingly explained that the Irish members pressed him to favour Kynoch'e, and he weakly yielded. How fair-Ininded, bow im- partial Though these men were traitors, pro-Boers, Nationalists, and what not, this Imperialist-Unionist Ministry did not wish to damage them whh their elec- tors. That Mr. Chamberlain's nearest blood relation— or, as he would call them, his " crenstituents "—were also interested in Kynoch's of course would not have in- fluened Mr. Powell Williams. But somehow we feel certain that if Kynoch's had,bren a Dutch firm run by Mr. Kruger's brother instead of Mr. Chamberlain's, the story would have been held to prove the hopeless cor- ruption of tha Boer Govern comt. 11.--HOSKINS.

MR. CHAMBERLAIN Illakl3-.ju-iiv-a-rs:-. His brother makes our cordite. But that is not the full extent of their in- terest In supplying the fighting services. Towards :he end of the year 1897, the business of Hoskins and Son, describing themselves as "CONTRACTOR'S TO THE ADMIRALTY," was for sale. It supplies berths, m , at- tresses shot and shell racks, and " all similar fittings for H. M. Navy "—we quote from one of the firm's ordinary letter headings, dated April, 1900. This busi- ness according to the latest register at Somerset House, is almost entirely in the hands of Mr Chamberlain's IM- MEDIATE FAMILY CIRCLE. Mrs Joseph Chamberlain holds 2,500 £5 Preference Shares, two of Mr Chamber- lain's sons hold 4,600 £5 shares between them. Two of his daughters hold 201 shares. It is not edifying to find that the family of Her Majesty's most prominent Minister is so largely ieterested in contracting for her navy. But the facts are even uglier. Mr Austen Chamber- lain, the son of the Colonial Secretary, himself a Civil Lard ot the Admiralty, is the owner of 600 £5 shares in this firm of" contractors to the Admiralty." That is against every sound principle of pure administration. Alien Mr. Austen Chamberlain, being already a Civil Lord of the Admiralty, ACQUIRED these shares, he must have known that he was doing a thing likely to demoralhe his department. Nor can we altogether ab- solve Mr. Joseph Chamberlain of a share in this scandal. Presumably he knew the facts. As a senior member of the Cabinet,he ought not to have countenanced AO grave a departure from the traditions of English public life. Taken together with the Kynech affair, this Hoskins revelation throws an ugly sidelight on the morals and motives bchiod Mr Chamberlain's policy of Imperialism and expansion. "THE EMPIRE," as Mr. Chamberlain once put it," IS COMMERCE" Members of the Chamberlain Family i also appear as largo holders of shares in the Birmingham Small Arms Company, Livnitvd, and in Tubes, Limited.

Both -cf these firms are contractors to the Govern- ment.

FewFRAL ELECTIO'

WARRINGTON. LIBERAL MEETING IN WHITECROSS WARD. Address by the Candidate, Mr. Crosfield and his Traducers St Barnabas Scathels were crowded on Tuesday even- ing when a meeting was held in support of the candida- ture of Mr A H Crosfield. Councillor Plumpton pre- sided and there were present on the platform, the candi- date, and Councillors James Evans, ;Burton. Tinniou, Peacock, Rev J Yonge, Mr W Furness, Mr T Hawthorn, and others. The CHAIRMAN observed that the lat. Government could have retained their office for neerly two years,but they tried to steal a march on the country by forcing the election upon them, and thus disfranchising some 400,000 more voters. ("Shame.") If it had been delayed until January next there would have been 400,000 voters, The only object for which this was done was because Mr Chamberlain was afraid to face the whole of the voters, yet only last week in Birmingham he stated that he was an advocate for every man having a vote. He did not know what con- fidence they could have 'inn a man of that sort now. He was afraid to face the consequences of his own work and that was the reason the General Election was taking place at the Present time. Mr. Crosfield said he Was quite as much an Imperialist in every way as Mr. Chamberlain—he oyed his Queen and country,and he would do everything he cauld for his country and fellow men. 0 (Hear, hear.) If they returned Mr. Crosfield they would return a man who would look after the interests and trade of the country. (Hear, hear.) If they returned Mr. Pierpoint they would return a man who was looking after th landlords and the Church. (Hear, hear.) They ought in their own interests, to send a man to Parliament who would look after the trade and industry of Warrineten, and not sand a man to look after the Cheshire squires and landlords. (Hear, hear.) Referring to the question of the taxation of ground rents he said that although the landlords were reaping the advantages which our in- dustrial system gave them in the form of increased rents they were not paying a single penny towards the rates of the town. (Shame.) If they paid taxes on the land the rates of the town could be reduced. :Speaking of the House of Lords he said it must be revised or done away With. (Cheers.) Mr. ARTHUR CROSFIELD, who had a flittering recep- tion, said : I am very sorry only got to bear a part o our Chairman's remarks, because ho was telling us some very important truths. He touched on two subjects that go home to every man who is a thoughtful Liberal and has the good of the community at heart. He touched upon the reform of the House of Lords. (Hear, bear.) gt will have to come gentlemen—(cheers)—in the interests of the country and that is one of the things I shall be most heartily glad to support. (Cheers.) Your chairman has also referred to the taxation of groun rents. That is only a matter of common justice. (Cheers.) I am not a hare-brained revolutionary but that is one of the reforms just as is that of bettermen which will have to be dealt with. It is a reform whic Will benefit every person in the whole country. (Cheers.) I am here te represent the old cause of Liberalism. (Hear, hear.) It is the cause which nearly a century ago my grandfather, Mr. Joseph Cros- field was fighting for—(cheere)—amid it certainly is the cause which for nearly 60 years my dear father has fought for. (Cheers.) The only single thing that depresses me in this contest is the thought that he is lying so unwell at home. That is the only thing that makes me sad. As for what my Tory friends tell me about the odds against me,l don't care one brass farthing for it. (Cheers.) I am sorry my father is not well enough to come on the platform and back us up, but I can assure you that his heart and soul are still with the Liberal cause, which without any clap-trap and party bunkum, makes for the good of the people. (Cheers.) The only other thing I am sorry about is the extraordinary tales my opponents are industri- ously circulatine about myself. I understano them to be that I have been an enemy to working- men from the point of view of labour. All can say is that from my youth up I have boon brought into contact with workingmen. I went to business at 17 and there mixed up with a large number of artizana and there are no people I have a greater respect for and no pecple from whom I have receivni greater kindness and tree in more thorough sympathy with than they. (Cheers ) As far as I am concerned as an employer of labour it is years since I took any charge of labour questions at the works. At one time I used to go round the soap pans and put my linen jacket on and whilst I don't say that I did not look after the interests of the employer of labour yet I can say With truth that I honestly tried to see the other side too. (Hear, hear). I have a thorough respect and thorough sympathyiwith Our Lancashire workingmen and so long as I have any- thing to do with public life I will do all I canito further the interests of workingmen and their pleasures also. (Cheers). I do believe and I have been trained in this achool—it is not a thing I have caught on for the elec- tion—that the greatest pride oflany country is not a few swell mansions but the number of healthy happy homes you get throughout the country. (Cheers.) Of course I am at one disadvantage in this election. That is time. I was unfortunately told whilst in America, by my friends here that if I started my campaign on the Ist October I should he in time. I suppose they thought the Government would be too straight-laced to spring this election too soon. You know it is a bit thick— laughter)—to deliberately disfranchiae 600,000 men in his country- (Hear, hear) I did not think they would have done it. I don't believe Lord Salisbury would have done so, but our pertinacious friend " Joseph" gets a good deal of his own way. (Laughter.) I don't think he is troubled with any great refinement of ecru- pe in these matters—(laughter)—and at all events he has done it and disfranchised 600.000 men. I think it Is a great shame. It is contrary, I think, to our consti- tution that it should be done without anyfneed of it. It is mere bunkum to pretend that there is any difference of principle as to the settlement of the;Seuth African question, which was the only plea for the election at this time. It him been sprung upon the country and the consequence is that our time has been cut very short and when to my amazement I hear of Conservative can- vassers representing Ins as a man who is an enemy to labour it leaves me very little time to nail that false coin to the counter. I can tell you that I am considered by my employers as always having erred; the other way. (Hear, hear.) That is the opinion of my friends. I could give you dozens of instances where they say I have let my sympathies with labour overrun my sense of regard for the difficulties of competition. ye no hesitation in saying that the statements which have been made that my actions have been inimical and un- friendly to those who have to do the hardest workjin this world is absolutely untrue. (Cheers.) I had not intended to say so much and I must beg yov pardon me having done so, but I did hear that a good deal was being said about it. Will you please tell your friends that is all bunkum ? (Cheers.) I would just like to ask you fair and square is there any single reason why the workingmen of this town should v ote against me ? (Cries of" No.") I don't honestly know one. On the other hand are there any reasons why they should vote for me and back me ? up (Cries of ' Yes.") I think there are. I don't want to boast as to being able to do too much. I never talk about bringing in the mil- lennium and doing the impossible. I believe in politics you ought to do as in business, and I will give you a good maxim and that is" Work for the best possible." One of the finest men of business I have ever met in my life, a man I have been competing with for 20 years and one of the finest men who ever trod the flags of Liver- pool,has that as his maxim. It does not do to attempt too much. It is no good trying to improve humanity off the face of the globe; you want to work for the best possible. There are a great many practical reforms that will benefit practically every man in this room, and I am here to represent them. (Hear, hear.) I haven't got much backing from the menied people and men of position, and I am sorry for it. I think it is a great pity, but, after all. I have to look the fact in the face. The issue on Thursday does not depend upon what these gentlemen are going to do or not to do, it absolutely rests in your own hands. Re- member that I am not standing here as a little Englander. Our interests at Bank Quay and our business with the whole world have driven into me the importance of keeping up the whole strength and pres- tige of our Empire. '(lear, hear.) Perhaps the train- ing I have bad from my father and my uncle George have helped on the other hand to counteract any undue tendency to what call rampant Imperialism. (Hear, hear.) I am sure you will aeree with me that rampant Imperialism is a very expensive game to say nothing else. (Hear, hear.) Although there may be a certain number of people now who would simply for the excitement of the thing favour what has been Called the operatic buffet business there are some ready for a fight for that object. Depend upon it that after a little while we shall begin to sober down because I am afraid there is in front of us a spell of not too good trade and there are some jolly big bills to be paid and all this kind of thing is going to come home. (Hear, hear.) It is the very reverse of what our Most respectable friends on the .other side led us to ex- Peet. I have read a great many politicel speeches—for I have always taken to politics like ducks to water—and I can remember the Duke of Devonshire, who is a very able man saying: We are in need of a period of rest. That was before the last Government came in I don't know whether he said retrenchment—(laughter)—but at any rate he said we wanted rest,quiet and peace all over the world; Well since the Conservative Government came into power we have had the very opposite and for my part I think we have had enough of it. (Hear hear.) The only strong point that I have heard the Tories allege why they should be supported again is the One that they have now got this South African business Oa hand and it is best to leave Mr Chamberlain to settle

it. I wil.i give him credit for firmness and courage, but I would just submit to you this consideratioa. You have got le South Africa much the samis class oi problem that you tad to deal with a great many years ago in Cenada. I have heard a great deal during my visit in America of the way in which Liberalllmeerialis- tic principles have helped to get the French and Eaglish to live harmoniously together and not simply by taking them by the throat. (Hear, hear.) We have got the upper baud in South Africa but there has got to be a little wisdom and a little judgment before you get those two great races there to settle down aril live quietly together. (Cheers.) If you ask me I am by no means certain that Mr. Clime- b?rlain is quite the type of statesman—l don't know whether he is a statesman in the true sense at all— (laughter)—to deal with this question. He is certainly a magnificent electioneerer—(laughter)—but if you want a real settlem-nt of the S nth African difficulty you want to give ihe Opposition and such men as Lord Rose. berv, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, Mr. Asquith and Sir Edward Grey a fair backing and a fair showing in the interests of the country. (Hear, hear.) Therefore I ask you not only to support me with your votes, but also to use your influence on my behalf. Lot me have a solid phalanx of support on the two grounds that we are not only the best possible party in domestic policy, but also because in foreign policy we can trust Liberal Imperialism. (Cheers). The Cetatremata proposed a resolution that Mr.Crosfield is a fit and proper person to represent the Borough in Parliament. (Cheers.) He remarked that Mr. Crosfield was deeply interested in the South Afrie,an affair, inas- much as he had a—brother fighting at the front at the present moment. (Cheers.) Their opponents told them that he had no interest in the South African war He might not have so much interest in it as Mr Chem- erlaia's family—(laughter)—but he was a man who, if sent to Parliament.would worthily represent Warrington and they needed level headed men like Mr. Crosfield to settle this question. He hoped they would do all they could to return Mr. Crosfield on the day of election. (Cheers.) Councillor PEACOCK, !ti seconding the resolution, said that es they had commenced the game let them play it well—(hear, hear)—sad show the Tories what they could do in Warrington as Liberals. Let them not only vote themselves but induc others to do the same. The resolution was put to the meeting and carried unanimously, three times three cheers being given for Mr. Crosfield, and the audience singing "For he's a jelly good fellow:" Mr CROSFIELD, in responding said it was an encour- agement to see such an exci-llent meeting. Since he landed in Eneland—when suddenly he got a telegram brought on to the steamer and was greatly surprised to find that the election was to be held so noon—he had done all he could because for about 14 or 15 hours a day he had been at it tooth and nail. He was :.fighting this battle of Liberalism to the beet of his ability. He could assure them that he was proud to carry the flag that his father and grandfather had carried before,him. (Cheers.) _ . . .

Councillor JAittss EvANs said that when they were landed in a fight they must take their corners. (near, bear The war in S exte Africa was foueht mainly on the question of the franchiie and when he came to think of two large number disfra rich 3 in this country by the present election, and that the war had cost over seventy milli one he thought their friend " Joseph could play a game when he liked. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Chamberlain was not the man to be trusted with the all-important things that con- cerned the workingman's bread and cheese. (fic ar, hear.) " When he begins to play the organ and tells me I have to pay, then I c:aitn I have a right to pick the tune." (Laughter.) At this election their opponents seemed to be altogether short of inspiration, and!short of something to say. The speaker proceeded to refer to the sneering remarks which had been made by Conservatives about democracy and werkingmen's repre- sentatives, and spoke with approval of the work of Mr. John Burns and Mr. Broadharst. Dealing with the slanderous statements regarding Mr. Crosfield and his workpeonle, he said he had made inquiries, and be had been unable to get any proof of the accusations brought against Mr. Crosfield. He found that the men who worke I at the Soapery got as much as labourers generally got anywhere else. (Hear, hear.) There were Tory firms in the town who bad not done am well for their workpeople RS Messrs. Crosfield'a had. (Hear. hear, and applause.) It would be to the interests of the town to have a repre- sentative in Parliament who was actively interested in its trade. (Hear, hear.) He (Councillor Evans) would be very sorry to hurt the feelings of Mr. Pierpoint, but it was well known that Mr. Pierpoint's interests in the town were very small. He would advise them to vote for Mr. Crosfield whose interests were identical with their own. (Cheers.) Councillor Timmer in the course of a short speech, said they bad a good meeting at Messrs. Crosfield's works that afternoon and he could say ,without fear of contradiction that the whole spirit of that meeting gave the lie direct to those gross and wilful mis-statements which had been made about Mr. Arthur Crosfield. (Hear, hear.) They had a fair opportunity of judging the real feeling of Messrs. Crosfield and Company's workpeople towards Mr. Crosfield personally. It all tended to prove that Mr—Arthur Crosfield was a great favourite with the workmen and had always been looked upon as a friend of labour and a friend of workingmen. (Hear, hear.) He only men- tioned this because he thought it was an impor- tant factor in the election- Mr. Crosfield was sound upon all Liberal principles and the Conservatives knew that the workmen were also sound politically. The only way they thought to damage Mr Crosfield in the eyes of the working-classes was to throw enough mud, thinking it would stick. Tales had been circulated in a way which made it almost impossible to contradict them. Mr Crosfield went to the works that afternoon in response to an invitation from the workmen's repre- sentatives, and it pleased him (the speaker) very much to hear Mr Cresfield state that he had taken the oppor- tunity of writing to Mr Robert Pierpoint that the direc- tors were prepared to find him facilities so that he could have the opportunity of addressing the workpeople if he wished to do so. It called forth the remark from one of the workmen that it was more than a Tory firm would do for the Radicals. (Hear, hear.) Although the Liberals might not .be ab'e to obtain a majority at the present election thoughoet the country he hoped that for the good of the country there would be a strong op- position. (Hear, hear.) If Liberal electors in Warring- ton would only put their shouldbrs to the wheel and do what in them lay, he was sure the result would be satis- factory to themselves and a credit to the good old town of Warrington. (Cheers.) The Rev. J. YoNGE, on being asked by the Chairman to come upon the platform and make a speech,said that in consequeece of the position which he happened to hold at the present time in connection with the Corpora- tion, perbaps he ought not to be very forward in politi- cal matters of a party character. Just now, however. he:had noticed on the posters issued bythe Conservative party, the names of some of his reverend brethren in the Church of England, and consequently they were known to be taking some part in the election, and h thought it would be an exceedingly unfortunate thing if no Nonconformist minister in the town put in an appearance at that meeting. He would like to say that inasmuch as the Liberals had determined to fight this election and had in- vited Mr. Crosfield to be their champion, it it behoved every member of the party to do all he could to carry it to a successful: issue. (Cheers). It was a very easy thing for them to comet° a meeting and hol up their hanJs in favour of the resolution but they must remember that electiens were not won by resolutions. They were carried by individual fidelity and individual industry, and by doing all that could be done to help their candidate to the position they wished him to occupy. This was to be a soldiers' battle and not an officers' battle. He remembered the time when they had a magnificent Liberal platform, including such men as Mr. Samuel Rigby, Mr McMinnies, Mr Peter Rylands, Mr John Rylands, Mr. Charles Broadbent, and he did not know bow many more leaders, but Providence hid removed them and at the present moment they had not got men of a similar stamp to occupy their places. But that was no reason why Liberalism should be dead in this Borough. (" N0,n0.") It was a strong reason why every workingman who felt himself a Liberal in his sympathies. should do the best he possibly could to hold up the Liberal standard and carry it to a glorious victory on Thursday. (Cheers.) The cause had his sympathy and he was glad to have had that opportunity of saying so. (Applause.) Councillor BURTON, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Chairman, said there was not a man in Warringtoa who held stronger Liberal views than himself. He very well remembered the gentlemen Mr. Yonge had referred to and he took part in some of the elections. He thought they should fight their hardest for the Liberal candidate on this occasion. He had known Mr. Crosfield from his youth up, and in a conversation that he had with him ten years ago he (the speaker), pre- dicted that Mr. Crosfield would some day be the Liberal candidate for Warrington. He was a man of whom Warrington ought to be thorou hly proud and one who was very much in sympathy with the artisans of this town. They in Warrington were largely dependent upon foreign trade, and they needed a representative in Par- liament who would look alter outlets for them. (Hear, bear.) He believed Mr. Arthur Crosfield was thoroughly suited for that kind of work. (Hear, hear.) I was our duty to keep our markets open and he believed that South Africa would prove a valuable colony to us. China had also been a great market to us. A large quantity of iron and ether materials had been S3nt to China from Warrington, and the present war had stopped the orders. If the Chinese market

Mr. CROSFIELD, who was received with prolonged cheering on rising to speak, said he thanked them very cordially fur the welcome that they had given him, and they could understand how extremely encouraging it was to himself to see such a fine meeting that evening ; and encouraging particularly when one had been told So often that this was going to be for him such an uphill fight. (Applause.) But he bad a feeling of the greatest pride that be was allowed by his Liberal friends to carry the same old Liberal Colours which not only his father but his grandfather fought for in this boroueln (Cheers.) It was his good fortune and his pleasure this year to be able to devote a good deal of his time to politics, and certainly, if they did him the honour to elect him to represent the borough he would endeavour to turn to the best account the ' business experience he had had and at the same time make politics the aim of his life. (Applause) He did not come before them as a little Englander or as an extreme faddist who wanted to tax capital out oE ex- istence, but simply and solely as a Liberal Imperialist with nothing to hide, and with the conviction that the interests of the people depended upon the furtherance of Liberal prin- ciples through aut the country. (Ap- plause.) Anything he could do for both in the future , he would be happy to do. The stories circulated were ' cock and bull stories and they wanted to go back to the real issues. It was absolute bunkum to say he was against the country on the war. His own brother was fighting there—(cheers)—and did they suppose his syrn- pathies and those of every member of the family were not with him ? (" No" and applause.) He would juet ask every workingman to consider what Liberalism had done for the country and to give him I a good solid backing. (Applause.) The issue rested in 1 I their own hands. He would not ask a single individual ! to vote for him who thought him in the wrong. (Cheers.) He not only asked for the votes of those present but they must also drive the true issues home and give him a solid phalanx of support which on Thursday would give their Tory friends the greatest surprise they had I had for years. (Cheers.) I Mr. JEFFERY moved that " this meeting considers Mr ' A H Crosfield a fit and proper person to represent War- rington at St Stephens." He said he had listened with great pleasure to the speech made by Mr Crosfield, and be thought it was quite sufficient recommendation for them to know: that he was a Crosfield. (Hear, hear). The speaker could remember the time when the working class had not the privilege of a vote, and he remembered Joseph Crosfield—(applause)—the good old champion of Liberalism advocating the claims of the workingman to a vote. Did they find any of the Conservatives or their descendants advocating the workingman's vote? No. , The electorshnust look at both sides of toe qeestion ! before them. On the one hand they had a Liberal—a 1 1 man of Liberal principles, of Liberal training and of ' Liberal proclivities, and on the other hand a Conserva- tive who had never been anything else but a Conserva- tive. The Conservatives had never done much for the people of this country—(hear, hear,)—and what they had ' done had been like the orange which had to be squeezed before they got anything out of it. Applause) If the working-classes would go back and study the history of the past hundred years they would find who had been their friend. The Tories claimed the Factory Act hut they only nave it under pressure. (Hear, hear.) The worleine-crasses of the country voted for that Act and the Liberals supported them and yet the Tories called it a Tory measure. They might as well say the repeal of the Corn Laws was a Tory measure, because Sir Robert Peel passed it, but Jahn Bright and Richard Cobden— (applause)—worked for it. Corning to present day topics let them go further. he said, and have "one man one vote? (Loud applause.) Was it right that Joseph , Chamberlain should be able to vote for one person in one place, and then be able to take train and vote for somebody else in another place ? ("No.") In conclusion bethought the epitome Mr. Crosfield had given them would convince all that they could not do better than put their shoulders to the wheel and by one great effort see if they could not repeat " one of McMinniee' jobs." (Cheers). Mr. WILLETT, an esteemed employee of Messrs. Cros- field and Sons for over 30 years, seconded the resolu- tion. Perhaps, he said, some would say he had been compelled to come on that platform and speak for Mr. Crosfield, hut that was not the case at all. They were not there to support the firm but Mr Arthur Crosfield himself. (Applause.) His Tory friends twitted him about things which they alleged were done at the works. The outside public seemed to know more about Cros- field's than the workmen themselves knew—(hear, hear) —and he could toll them honestly that Crosfield's firm was no worse than a good many in Warrington. (Ap- plause.) They were not as bad as they were painted, and ho thought the Tories might keep such assertions to themselves. (Hear, hear.) That was one thing off his mind. (Loud applause.) Five years wee the Tories asked why they could not bring out a local man to con- test the borough, and now they had got one. (" Here is the man " pointing to Mr. Crosfield.) (Cheers.) What more did they want ? Mr. Crosfield was an Imperialist,— as true a one as ever Mr. Pierpoint was. (Applause.) He was at one with his opponent on the war question. That day they had had a meeting at the works which Mr. Crosfield had known nothing about until he was asked by the men to put his views before them, and it was understood that no man appearing at that meeting thereby gave any promise as Ito which side he was going to vote. Mr. Pierpuint was welcome to have a meeting at the same place, and speak from the same platform. (Applause.) The speakeralhad been at Crosfield's works 34 years, and he would defy any man to prove that Mr. Crosfield, during the time he was at Bank Quay, ever did a mean or unjust thing. (Applause.) He was always the readiest to put his hand in his pocket and help a deserving cause. (Applause.) He was a true sportsman who believed in enjoying him- self and in helping others to enjoy themselves at bowls, cricket, or anything else. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. Crosfield had always been the one totsupport the working classes, and he would always support the reforms in their interest. (Haar, bear.) They wanted better housing for the poor, and not the dingy holes some poor folk had to live in—(applause)—and if they voted for Mr. Crosfield on Thursday they would never have cause to regret it. (Applause). Mr. B. FARRAR supported the resolution, and proff- ered Mr. Crosfield his hearty !support, and ho hoped every man in the room would do likewise. He was opposed to the action of the Government in plunging them into this contest at the present time. No minister however strong had a right to force it upon the country for any party motive, and he thought the action of the Government one of the dirtiest actions ever .done in the , name of the Tory party. (Applause.) He was sere if , they prepared themselves to do their level best the result , would be a surprise, and a terrible surprise to their opponents. Ho was proud that they were supporting a man who would be loved by everybody. (Applause.) ' The Conservatives themselves admired Mr. Crosfield and ' were sorry they would have to go against him. (Applause.) Questions on Home Rule and Old Age _ Pensions.

An Irish elector asked Mr. -Crasfleld what position he took up with regard to Irish questions and Home Rule in particular. He thouelat Ireland was being left out in the cold and the Irish vote was worth being recognised, (Hear, hear.) Mr. CROSFIELD replied that as to Ireland beine shelved there was no one less Zinclined to agree to that than he ‘eas. He asked his questioner to consider which party had made enormous sacrifices on behalf of Ireiand. (Hear, hear.) Let him consider the great sacrifices made on behalf cf Irish reforms by the Grand Old Man. (Hear. hear.) Home Rule was the last of all. It was the efforts of Mr. Gladstone and the Liberal Party which had made the lot of the Irish agriculturalists what it was to-day—better than hat of English agriculturalists. The Irish (-tight to back them to a man in this contest. (Applause.) He could give no pledge to vote kr Home Ride in the next Parliament, because it was no use their running their heads aeainst a stone wall. (Hear, ta-ar.) In polities and ether walks of life they had to work" for the best possible." It would be absurd, with opinion in the couatry as it was now, to introduce Home Rule at present, but very likely in years tn come they would find Home Rule introduced in some modified form, and it may be brought about by that very Imperialism to whiel he was pledged. The enormous amount of work entailed upon the mother country made it necessary that something in the nature of Home Rule must be accorded Ireland. He asked every Irishman in the town to remember what friends the Liberal Party had been to Ireland. (Hear, hear.)

, Mr. PEMBERTON questioned Mr. Crosfield with regard to the Ofd Age Pen,ion Scheme. The liEt Government gave a oef,e pronfse to do something in the matter, but they had absolutely failed. (Applause.) Was Mr. Crosfield prepared to support a measure which would grant old men reasonable supporti? The £25,000,000 increase of taxation 1a,4 year was sufficient to have given every man over es years of age 6i a week. (Hear, hear.) Mr. CROSFIKLD said that when the Old Age Pension Scheme was dealt wi:11 he thoughts they ought to dis- criminate between the wastrel and the sober and thrifty person. They should not be on the same footing. (Ap- plau3e.) Mr. Charles Booth's scheme was to give every- body over 65 years of ago 5s a week, which would cost

the country 17 millions a year. Hero they were ape ling 60 or 70 millions upon war, and the 17 millions L . no chance whatever. (Applause.) Personally he would like to support a measure which would be a blow to the Friendly Societies, but he certainly was not opposed to an Old Age Persion Scheme. It was one of four or five other problems that he had asked his Liberal friends not to pin him down to in detail as he could riot pretend to master the whole field at politics. He requested them to give him a chance of exchanging opinions, with some of the best brains in the country. (Applause.) Let him go and thrash these quesJous out and he would be delighted to come to Warrington and thrash them cut with the electors. (Loud applause) Finally he would say he would not be opposed to any reasonable scheme for assisting necessitous people in old age. i. (Applause). The meeting shortly afterwards dispersed, Mr. Cross field being heartily cheered as he drove away.

On Tuesday evening another meeting in support of Mr. A. EL Crosfield's candidature Wit. 3 !Jed in Brick- street schoolroom, which was crowded to its utmost capacity by electors who had not only come to listen but to support the Liberal candidate. The Chairman's Speech;

The CHAIRMAN in the course of his opening remarks said the fight in Warrington would; not be complete unless someone bearing his name took a part in it— (applause)—and one qualification that he had for pre- siding over the meeting was that he was the solitary representative of that ward in the Borough Council. Creatinuing the speaker said that Mr W J Shaw had sent Mr Crosfield a series of questions bearing on the liquor traffic and that gentleman had the effrontery to say that Mr Crosfield's answers would decide the vital ques- tion as to whether he and his brother publicans would vote for him. Did they believe anything on this earth would iaduce Mr W J Shaw and his brother publicans to vote for Mr Crosfield or any other Liberal. (Cries of "No "). Then what had they to say to such an invita- tion as that? Did they they think it was bona fide; he did not. He (the speaker) had been in that fight from the outset as Mr Crosfield's election agent, and he knew something about how it was going on. He thought perhaps they would like to 3 know what their prospects were. When they began that ght everyboay said they were making great fools of themselves. (Laughter,) Their own leaders said it wa almost quixotic proceeding and Mr. Crosfield had not the shadow of a chance. He (the speaker) was not a fighting man hut this he would say that if he had got to be licked he would not lie down to it. (Laughter and applause). There were a few who thought it a great mistake te allow Mr. Pierpoint to have a walk over. They did not, of course, know exactly what Mr. Cros field would say on the question for he was some thou- sands of miles away. But they knew Mr. Crosfield was one of those gentlemen who had resisted all the temptations of wealth and position to back up the sacred course of freedom and the prosperity of workingmeaa He had resisted these temptations and remained as true and loyal al Liberal as any man in Warrington to-day. They knew Mr. Crosfield had given them a general assurance that whatever the Executive Committee decided to do he would firmly and loyally abide by it and when he came back he said "Gentlemen you have done quite right. I don't expect to be returned member for Warrington by a majority of a thousand. It may possibly be tha I shall be defeated but I will fight if I am defeated by 1,500 votes, to show that in my opinion the Liberal party should make a protest against the gross misman agement of the nation's affairs by the present Conserva- tive Government." (Applause.) That was the spirit they wanted and although some of them were not in favour of a fight, they resolved to go on. The Conservatives were beginning to wonder what mischief they were after. They thought the Liberals were working underground. They had not had many big meetings and the Conserva- tives had got an uneasy feeling that there was something mischievous going on. To tell them the solemn truth there;was. (Laughter.) The Conservatives had been making a great show of local names on their placards, and he (the speaker) must admit that the Liberals bad been left very severely alone by the so.called leaders of the Liberal party. They had not many coming to their assistance, and one gentleman went so far as to say that he did not think the seat would ever be won by the men who were taking the lead in the present cantest. Well why did not he and other men like him come to take the Lead? (Hear, tvar and applause.) lie and others did not want to take the lead, or to push themselves forward but they were not goiug to leave the thing to nobody.
