MR. GORDON RYLANDS AND THE CONTEST.

The Issue before the Electors.

R . ROBERTS producei a letter from the late Mr. Peter lands' eldest son. Mr. Gordon Rylands. (Applause.) It put in a nutshell the Liberal cause. The letter was as folio

- "Dear Arthur Crosfield. I have been taking a great interest in your caadidature and heartily wish you success in your plucky fight at Warring- ton. It is strange if workingmen have any doubts as to the party which they should support at this election. The Conservatives got into power by making abundant promises, but during the five years they have been in office they have done very little for the workingman; on the contrary they have assisted their own friends at the working- man's expense. When they came into power they found a full exchequer, tranquility abroad and cor- dial relations between Great Britain and the other European nations. They had been in power but a very short time when foreign complications began to occur and we have had a war scare nearly every yearisince. The result of their five years' rule is that we are hated and distrusted by all our neigh- bours, and we are very heavily in debt. To crown all, after going to war to obtain the franchise tor Outlanders in South Africa, they have deliberately choben to fix the General Election at such a time that :many thousands of workingmen are disfran- chised, owing to the state of the register. Had they waited even three weeks longer this mighChave been avoided. The question for the electors to decide now is not whether the war in South Africa was necessary or not, but whether, on the record of the past five years, the present Government is fit to be trusted to manage the basiness of the nation. 1 sincerely trust that the electors of Warrington will answer" no" to that question by voting for you on Thursday. (Applause.) I am deeply grieved to hear of your fatheee illness." Mr. ROBERTS, continuing, said it was one of his great- est regrets that their old friend sold lea ler, Mr. John Crosfield, was too ill to help his son. He (the speaker) had the pleasure of seeing In on Sunday night and he knew his heart was with them and it was a bitter regret to him that he could not render his *ma) assistance. S 3 they would see that they were not absolutely left to themselves in this hour of their supposed extremity. One great objection to ttne, Liberals at the present mo- ment, was that they were little Englanders or alleged to be. Now, for the life of him, ,he had never been able to understand how a man who,it was thought, monopo- lized all the patriotism of the time, should under any circumstances bring himself to call &viand little. It seemed to him that it was a contradiction in terms. The Liberal party never had been little Englanders. It was to the Liberal party,gthat we owed all our present colonies. Address at Bank Quay Soap

Works.

At the veriest of the representatives of Messrs. Cros- field and Sens workmen, Mr. A 11 Crosfield visited the works on Tuesday and addressed the employees. In the course of his remarks he said : I should like to say in the first instance that the very first thing I intend to do immediately I return to the Patten Arms is to send a note to my friend Mr. Pierpoint, to tell him that if he cares to come and address you he shall have every opportunity of doing so. (Cheers.) He and I are ex- ceedingly good friends, and apart from that I have always taken, well I have always, I won't say taken pride, but been particularly careful that politics shall not create the siightest bias or favour one way or the other in the case of any man in the concern, and it won't make any difference to my personal feelings to anybody connected with the con- cern—(cheers)—whether they vote for rue or against me. I dare say you know what I have come for. (Laughter.) I have come to ask you, so far as your political convictions will allow you, to give me all pos- sible support and backing in the contest I have under- taken with a considerable handicap owing to the short time at our disposa.l. May I particularly ask my lady friends here to we their inflaence in my favour also ? (Cheers.) Referring to some of the statements circulated about him he said.—lf any people are going about Warrington saying I am against the country on this war, I ask everyone of our employees who cares to see fair play— and I am sure whatever your opinions may be you do not want to see Me handicapped uufairly—(hear, hear and cheers)—to nail that bit of false coin to the counter. (Hear, hear.) Is not my own brother fighting there, and do you suppose that the sympathies of every mem- ber in our family?are not with him. (Cheers.) I have criticised the methods that were employed before the war, but, as I have said, since the first shot was fired there has been nothing for it but to fight; It has been absolutely necessary that British arms should triumph since it came to fighting, and there has been nothing for it but this annexation, and if some of you do not approve of all that went on before the war took place, let us hope that the end will justify the means, and thlt the well-being of those countries under English rule may be a happy solution of the difficulties that still lie before it. I am told that there are some amusing fictions being circulated about me through the:town in the canvass, and what I want to say is this, that as regards the control of labour here, it is a good many years since that ceased to be within my province, but formerly I was closely connected with the work and wages of a good many men in this place and looking back to that time I venture to hope and believe that if you ask the older hands who knew me in those days—those who are with us now and those who have left—l don't think you will find them bringing one single charge against me. I have always tried to look at both sides of the question, and to deal impartially and fairly, and I have been brought up from my youth in a democratic circle where I always felt a keen interest in the welfare of those who have the hardest work in the world, and have done any thing in the direction of sport I could to further their interestq and their welfare. (Applaim).

Mr. WILLETT sub ,mitted a resolution as follows : •That Mr. A. H. CrosfiPld is a fit and proper person to represent the town of Warrington in Parliament." This was seconded by Mr. RIGBY, and carried with enthusiasm.

Open-Air Meeting at Wilderspool,

Mr. A. H.CROsFIELD addressed the workmen em- ployed at Aessrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.'s during the dinner hour on Monday. Councillor PLUMPTON introduced the candidate and said he had never seen any better machinery in any works in Warrington than he had just seen at Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.'s works. He asked the workmen to vote for and use their influ- ence on behalf of the Liberal candidate. Mr. Cros- field would be an excellent representative of the trade and commerce of Warrington. (Applause.) Mr. CROSFIELD, who had a cordial reception,said he was greatly indebted to the directors of the con- cern for allowing him to address the workmen, and he thanked the latter for giving him an opportu- nity of saying a few words to them. He hoped they would be able to hear what he had to say, but since he landed in Eng!and—for the last 60 or 70 hours—he had been talking 16 to the dozen, and he had not, therefore, a great deal of voice left. But he hoped soon to be all right again. They knew what The had come to the works for—to ask for their vote and support. He was standing as a Liberal, and he knew that very often in the contest they had heard it said that there was no differ- ence between Liberalism and Toryism. In the , matter of the foreign policy there was much less difference than there used to be be admitted, but in the matter of domestic legislation there was considerable difference still, and if they asked him what Liberalism really consisted in now it was all based on the grand old formula of John Locke "The end of oovernment is the goodiof mankind." He did not think that any in- telligent and rsflecting artisan in Warrington, or in any other town, could need any more illustration of the op- posite form of government that th.y had in the last Administration. (Rear, hear.) He knew that the in- terests of the whole community were not found in tar- ing capital and in robbing people or playing any foolish pranks of that kind. He was no socialist or faddistibut a Liberal to the backbone. He had been broueht up on it, and be knew how it affected the welfare of tie' people at large, and also that the prosperity and great- ness of a 'country depended mainly upon the num- ber of healthy, happy hlmes that they got throughout the land. His faith in Liberal principles was as deep and as strong to-Jay as over it was. He knew per- fectly well that when be came back to Warrington they would ask why he had not come back a bit sooner. It was represented to him in America that if be started the campaign on the Ist October it would be all right, and he did not think that the Government would have played such a trick as to disfranchise 600,000 voters in the kingdom. If the Liberals had done such a thing they would have been called by every name in creation. That was not a thing that would meet with their approval. At any rate the General Election was not anticipated, and he was advie9d to come back and open his cam- paign on the let Oetober. Of course very little time was left for him to prepare for the contest. He knew perfectly well when he began' that the odds,so to speak, would be against him, but he did not mind that a bit. He stood on the chance of winning and he stood for Liberal principles, and he cared nothing for any odds if he only got a fair backing from the men for whom he was fighting. He would spend his time and do every- thing that was necessary, but he contended that he was entitled to that backing. Lord Rosebery had said it was a bad thing to have "all the haves on one side and all the have not.; on the other." They had to deal with facts. He (Mr. Crostiold) was left almost alone on his platform as regarded the men of wealth and high posi- tion in this neighbourhood. They had to deal with facts and the facts were these—that he was left. They only found a small snrinking going -rith them, but he dared say it was a little leaven that leavened the whole lot. (Laughter.) Still it was only a small sprink- ling. The Liberals had fought for a whole century the battles of tile artiztn, and he relied entirely upon the intelligent aristocracy of labour as he preferred to call it. They should join shoulder to shoulder, and not only use their influence in these works,but,he hoped,through- out the whole of the town in his favour. He knew that the other side were counting on having a j soft thing on, but if his friends amongst the working classes—and he had no enemies amongst them—would do as he had said, and work shoulder to shoulder, back him up, and go into the fight as honestly and as earnestly as be should go into it himself, without any cor founded clap- trap or nonsense, they would give the other side a very great surprise on Thursday. He had only two short things to say to them in conclusion because he might not trespass upon their time in the works. The Tories were trying to make some capital out of South Africa, and talked about loyalty to the Empire. Of all the bun- kum that ever was that was it. He had his own brother fighting there, and did any of them sup- pose that the sympathies of every member of the family were not absolutely heart and soul with him in the work in whieh he was engaged ? They were waiting for every mail which brought news from him, and far as that went he (the speaker) absolutely concurred in the only possible alternative of the war, and that was the annexation7of the country. There were placards in the town advising the electors to vote for his friemd Mr. Pierpoint, and about being loyal to the Empire, but he (Mr. Crosfield) was as loyal to the Empire as Mr. Pierpoint was. Had he been a little Eoglander he would not have been there at all. He was to the backbone a Liberal Imperialist, and he could only tell them it was the old story of throwing dust in their eyes and all clap-trap and non- sense. There was not a single issue on which the work- ! ingmen of Warritigton were not thoroughly entitled to i support him. There was nothing on which he really

differed from them, anl there were many things wbere they had had in the past, and had now, a far greater and a far more honest claim upon the voters than the other side had. Their reforms were the sugar plums thrown to them as a S)p to Cerberus. The Conservatives did not go in for what was really for the benefit of the people for the sake of it being a benefit to the people. There was too much trickery about it altogether. He thanked them very much for having given him the chance of saying a few words to them,and he was sorry if he had spoken too strongly. He was speaking to a section of the community in Warrington to whom he looked for his best support, and he hoped they would give it him. (Applause.)

Tory Promise and Perform-

ance.

A Lesson from the Compensation

Act.

Mr. Chamberlain's Promise (at the General Election, 1895).

" While he (Mr. Asquith) will deal with this subject incompletely, inefficiently, I propose to deal with it once for all and completely. I prorwe to say that every workman who is injured without fault of his own shall be entitled, as a matter of right, to reasonable compensa- tion, to such solace in his misfortune as pecuniary assist- ance can give, and I propose not to make that a question of Litigation, not to send him into the courts to get it, but to make it a matter of statute, about which there can be no possible uncertainty," Workman (minus a leg) : What happened to me? Scaffoldin' gave way and I fell off the top of the house. Compensation ? I didn't get a ha'porth—the lawyers said as how I didn't come within the Act, because the buildin' was only 29 feet 11 inches high. Workman (with crippled arm) : That's bad luck. I only fell a matter of 15 feet,but I got £lOO at last after the lawyers had tried it three times. How was that ? Well they found out the builgin' was just 30 feet 6 inches high, and that brought me within the Act, so they said. It's a rum Act. ain't is ? There's poor Jack Hodge bein' buried to-day. He was killed on Mr. Smith's farm and his widow don't get a farthin' ! How's that ?

Well, you see, he was only an agricultural labourer, and they don't count accordin' to the Act.

What a Tory M.P. Thought of the Compensation Act.

Tbe late Sir Arthur Forwood, a Tory Liverpool Mein. her, said in June, 1898 "From the benefits of the Act of last year the people who were the least organised, the met poorly paid, and. the least able to take care of themselves were excluded." Could there be a more scathing cormlemnation ? How the two parties voted on the proposal to include farm labourers within the Compensation Act.

THE LATE MR JAMES SUM MERSGILL. Forty Years' Record as Local Preacher.

On Sunday evening, Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel, wascrowded on the occasion of a memorial service to the late Mr. James Summerseill,nf Burtonwood-road,Sankey. who was practically the founder of Methodism in tbe vil- lage and was for upwards of 40 years,a most acceptable local preacher in Warringten and other circuits. The Rev. John Gunnell was the preacher, and during the evening very suitable hymns were sung and two special selec- tions were rendered by the choir.'4lr Gonnell took as the subject of his discourse, Rev. 21c. 4v. " And there shall be no more pain.". He remarked that the members of Sankey Bridges Chapel were under a threefold cloud. Mr. Summersgill in whose memory they had met that night was the first. In the middle of the week. Mr. Isaac Hough, a member of the choir and Sunday School had passed away, and that morning a young man who was a member of the choir and also a teacher in the Sunday School hag died. The deepest sympathy of every one present would go forth to the bereaved families. At the close of the service, Mr Gunnell gave a brief sketch of the life of Mr Sumneerseill. He said that the latter was born in Oldham in 1833 His father and grandfather were local preachers of god repute in the Methodist Church, and his early associations were thor- oughly Christian and had much to do with the function of that character which they had all learned to respect. His family left Oldham and went to reside at Middleton when he was six years of age. He attended the Sunday school there and at the are of 14 years, he was soundly converted and he early began to show a singular apti- tube for preaching. He was not however a thoroughly accredited local preacher until he came to Warrington in 1860. Several still remembered the credit with which he passed his theological and biblical examination and the good report of his trial sermon. For several years he was in the town and while diligent in business, he was fervent in spirit serving the Lord. He frequently occupied pulpits of this and neighbouring circuits and was the leader of a large society class being held in very high esteem. Mr Summersgill came to Sankey Bridges in 1875. At that time there was only a sparse popula- tion and Methodism was then only feeble. There was no chapel but services were held for many years in the house of the village poomtster,Mr Eakdale. There was a society class led by Mr.Eakdale.hut there was eo Sunday school. On Mr. Summeragill's arrival at Sankey Bridges, he set about with characteristic energy to prc- vide a new chapel and Sunday School for the wants of the growing neighbourhood. The old iron chapel was erected, and Mr. Summersgill collected himself every subscription promised fer that erection, which was opened in Sentember, 1876. At the opening services there was still a debt of £6O, and Mr. Summersgill, who was the first local preacher to occupy the pulpit, told his wife on the preceding Wednesday that he would not preach before every penny had been secured. He set to work at once, anti at the Sunday morning service he Was able to announce the joyful news to the congrega- tion that the place was free from deht, thus collecting £6O in four days. When the Sunday school Was opened there were 15 present in the morning and 25 in the after- noon. but it soon became crowded, and nine years after it had grown to 200. The memhersle:p had also grown from 8 to 60. and though in 1851 vestries were added, these failed to provide accommodation for the increas- ing congregations. Mr. Summersgill threw his whole soul into the work of providing a new chapel and be had the pleasure of laying the foundat tion stones of a new building. He also collected- a large proportion of the money required. One of the gladdest days of his life was March 2nd, 1885, when the opening aervices of the present chapel were presided by the Rev Charles Garnett who amid the greatest enthus- i,sm dedicated the place to the service of Almighty God. Some years later Mr Sernmergill retireu from business and went to live in Burtnnwood-road, when his life necame one of comparative retirement. For well-nigh 40 years, he had delighted in the business of the chapel, and he was entitled to a li'tle relaxation. There was still one more scheme and that was; the erection of new schools.Mr Summerseill gave generously to thin and took his share of the responsibility, but he Was satisfied that youngerimen should take the more prominent part of the work. If ever the history of Sankey Bridges Methodism were written the name of Mr Summersgill would have a very prominent place in it. As a preacher he was thoroughly evanvelical very peaceful in his style, and very earnest in his manner. He had his defects. Who had not ? In all of the effiees he served the chapel faith- fully and well. The end was very painful and bet for the most skilful medical treatment would have been far greater. He bore his sufferings with the greatest patience and with wonderful resignation. Again and again he assured him (Mr Gunnell) that he was on the Rock and the end came very swiftly, and he passed away from this life without a struggle.

So many palpably die- Some Election honest election cries are Cries.

in use that it may be , as well to take one or two of th em, and show their I falsehood. It is said that Liberals are "traitors" to their country because one or two Liberals have written certain letters. It would be just as hon- ourable to urge t hat the Conservatives are traitors because Sir EDW ARD CLARKE trounced Mr CHAMBERLAIN for his conduct of the negotiations, land Commander BETH ELL objected to the war, and Mr. COURTNEY has done his best to represent the cause of the Boers in a favourable light. The use of the terra traitor is disho nest, objectionable, and vituperative. The Liberals are declared not to be "patriots " because their attitude in the early part of the n egotiations was critical, and they have objected to Mr. ,CHAMBERLAIN'S irritat- ing, suspicious, and offensive references to the man and people with who in he was ostensibly seeking to arrange nutters in South Africa. It was a Liberal Go vernment that gave back the Transvaal. But in that Government were the DUKE of DEVONSHIRE and Mr. CuAmBERLAIN.They have repented, and that is now the attitude of many of their opponents. The Boers were given a chance,and they never made the best of it. The first denial of what we expected of them was made under Lord _SALISBURY'S Government:in :11890-1 when, without any protests, the Boers of the Transvaal contracted the political right of British and other Outlanders. " Remember Majuba," shout the Conservatives. Bat they forgot it in 1890-1, and during the years 1895 to 1899 they permitted the Transvaal to arm for the struggle, and took no steps to prevent it. If the Transvaal had not been well armed, Mr. KRUGER would have given way. It was not two or three ietters that made him obstinate ; it was the consciousness of his military strength, and that had been acquired during.the time Mr. Ca AMBERLAIN was Colonial Secretary, and when effective me asures might have averted the sanguinary strife which has cost us so many lives and so many m illions of money. Another cry, equally false, is that " every vote given to a Liberal is a vote for the Boers?' It is a wilful and malicious misrepresentation, intended to catch the votes of the ignorant. Mr. CHA MBERLAIN,WhO has been flinging so many of these accusations broad- cast with his characteristic indifference to accuracy and his mad desire to win votes, has also said that if the Liberals came into power annexation of the Republics would not last "six months." Inas- much as the Liberals accept annexation as an accomplished fact such a statement can only be properly de scribed by an objectionable monosyll" able. Mr. BALFOUR'S cry about the Conservatives being true army reformers has been already disposed of in these columns. His promise of Roman Catholic education changes has secured Salford for the Conservatives, but he must be well aware that he will not succeed in carrying any measure of the kind. It is like MT.CHAMBER- LAIN's old-age pension cry at the last election—a mere catch-vote paltering with public policy.

Parliament will never endow the Roman Catholic Church with a university out of national money or any other educational scheme. The cry about "Little Englanders" is about as bad as the others, and its effect in Northampton and Battersea has been what we might have expected. Much has been said about promoting union with the Colonies. It has never been endangered by any but Tory policy. Self-government, given to theca by Liberal statesmen, has made them devoted to the mother country. As long as they were Crown Colonies their loyalty was limited. When it was proposed to endow them with self-government the Tories said the Liberals meant them to detach and cut themselves off. The old argument is now seen to be as false as current ones. The Empire is in no danger except from aggression, wastefulness and incompetency at home. Exhaustion and aggression are the two things to fear. Insolence and swagger about "the flag" are far more likely to imperil the Empire than Liberalism, good government and peace.

ON the Continent the ,-_As Others See Us. General Election here is held to explain our dip- lomatic uncertainty in China. Lurd SArassußS does not promptly meet the new phases as they arise,and he issupposed to be unwilling to act with energy until he sees how the elections are going. This is an interesting state of affairs, but the ex- planation is not sound. Lord SALISBURY has been always weak and dilatory ever since the Chinese question became acute. A change of Government or a marked change of feeling in the country would be regarded as promising better results and more firmness and prevision. We have lost our strong position in China. Have we lost ground on the Continent during the war? If we may believe CHAMBERLAIN We are more hated DOW than ever we were—because we are strong! The point is important. The reports of the military attaches who have studied the South African War on the spot are not at.all in our favour. It was intended to appoint General GRENFELL to the command of the Allied troops in China. But the idea had to be given up, and our readers will remember hovi suddenly it was dropped. Captain GAMBLER, explains in the Fortnightly how it was. Our mill' tary prestige was believed to have been so injured during the South African War that the concert would have been broken up if the headship of an English General has been pressed. The Allies were unwilling, they did not consider it safe, to be led by an English Generalissimo. It cannot he contended that anybody but the Government is responsible for this reflection upon our Dallas*? skill. The hack story about the deficiency of ea- dite small-arm ammunition will not account for the disposition to regard us as an inferior military Power. Continental countries noted our obsolete artillery, our muddling methods, and our bad generalship, and they were alarmed. It is a sad business. Turning from this singular incident, which the Government speakers carefully ignore, to the general conduct of the war, what do we find? That military men abroad are very severe on the way in which our generals clung to the rail- ways, made frontal attacks, and were unable to beat two non-military Powers with three or four times the number of trained soldiers in the field. When they were cut off from the main body our troops surrendered instead of entrenching them selves, and holding on until relieved. Too much made of these surrenders, no doubt; but atill the' revealed an amount of ignorance and incaPacity the part of officers which is the reverse of Pleas° Our trenching tools were not carried by the Wen who needed them, and even on Spion KoP th° men in tho advance had to wait for then Egineers: Such critici3ms are galling to our pride, bt.ljv we cannot brush them away. They Wei lowered our military prestige in the eYes Continental nations. They give emphasis t° tithe need for thorough reform in our militia,' system. Expenditure alone may be increased without putting everything right. As Sir 11• - CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN has said, we must deulcr cratise the army, and offer careers to capable roe° and pay them better. Our military as well as our political constitution breaks down 011 the aristocratic side. This is what other countries are seeing and saying of us. When we come t° technical and commercial education, the sat° js fault-finding is observed. We are being outPacs' and the quest for new markets, upon which 10' CHAMBERLAIN lays such stress,will be of little ger" vice if we are losing the old ones. The reason is that our methods lack science, skill, and organiase. tion. The Germans are beating Us. What refit help is likely from, any but democratic sources! Some Unionists thinks more of :catechisms than °lg commerce. Surveying the general position °I Great Britain, our foreign critics are by no meal° as friendly as they used to be. They resPee° strength, but theyideride weakness, and just noW our weakness seems to them the one thing to ba commented upon. If electors will dwell on thess. points it will assist them in voting in a patriotic way.

Sir John Brunner is not likely to be oust.ed, from his seat as representative of the Northvac'd division. He is too strong a man politically ail. 6 morally for that, and his opponents know They, however, are doing their best to discredit John in the eyes of the electors, and in a fashl°ll„ too, which is " playing it rather low " as they 5% in America. Allegations of Pro-Boer and Lil Englander proclivities have been common Luau', tions of warfare on the Conservative side,and tb have to their eternal discredit not hesitated countenance lying insinuations about Sir Brunner's treatment of his workpeople. The thing, to a; much less extent, has been done Warrington, and Mr Croslield has promptly nail° ;. the false coin to the counter. Sir Join Bruhpei has done likewise, and it will not now pass nalistea in the Northwich division. Strange to say, ait t, meeting in support of the Conservative eandit",- at .Runcorn on Wednesday, Mr linisoup chairman, accused the _Liberals of 0-171-10 clueing personalities. This is shifting guilt on to other shoulders with vengeance. We admire Mr. Imisou's elfronterY u 1.1,49 not his veracity. He also stigmatised 711 mean, the making of capital out of purso,llo- - What an idea ! 'When a niarl.h, endeared himself to a community, as Sir iO Brunner has by countless acts of kindness, his c't—of vassers are mean if they refresh the ineult)rYgr the elector on some of these matters. Such 10 Imison's conviction, and we are sorry for whose ideas are warped to such an extent. .-1-" a electors will look at the matter differently, W° 8r sure, and " vote for Brunner" !

was the ieen

terms of praise used by him we are willing to admit that the oratory as a whole was not bad— for after-dinner speech-making. Some men spoke better than others, naturally, but the speech of the evening was certainly that delivered by the Mayor, who displayed a characteristically close acquaint- ance with his subject and spoke briefly and to the point. He is entitled to some elation at the honour conferred upon the town during his Mayoralty, and especially as it is to a large extent due to his own persistence. No one has recognised more than Alderman Roberts, how much the town was crippled by the vets of the Lancashire County Council. In Technical Education particularly, progress and expansion were almost impossible owing to the injustice and meanness of the paramount authority, but now all this is at an end, and the good ship Warrington floats on an even keel, with its own captain at the helm. The new responsibilities are fully realised, and we look to an era of municipal development and commercial progress which will justify—if any further justification were needed— Warrington's severance from the County Council of Lancashire.

Every newspaper editor is painfully conscious of the stimulus war gives to versification; while the conductors of the magazines which regularly lend a portion of their space to poets have been burdened during the last few months to an extent beyond any previous experience. The net result to the public is, Kipling's "Absent-Minded Beggar " linos with plenty of go, a spice of vulgarity, nowa- days as much needed as the suspicion of onion used to be for the salad bowl ; and with a fine dis- regard of grammar. Mr Alfred Austin has strained after effect until the flesh has been worn off his soldiers, and only their hard bones shew. Mr. W. E. Henley has made one effort, a staccato composi- tion with an annoying sense of cleverness. Mr. Thomas Hardy did better with his elegy over a soldier buried in South Africa. Mr Swinburne strangely enough, has confused poetry and politics and Mr Henry Newbolt, the most promising of the rising generation, has not helped us. For the rest, we know from the decimal percentage that the editors have allowed t) pass into print the flow has been one consistent stream of doggerel, sometimes maudlin sentiment and sometimes remarkable as decent prose gone wrong. No wonder that the cry is raised in this month's "Nineteenth Century," " Wanted a New War Poet." The kinetic power of imagination to put together in a single picture the stress and pathos, the courage of resignation, the enthusiasm and the ,quiet endurance which lighted up and shadowed the score of memorable compaigns seems to be lacking. Perhaps it is the fault of the newspapers in over-loading the mind with details, and so blinding to the broad, great features of the war. What is needed is a Munskacsy among the poets!

The fact that the General Election is to la() short and sharp, whether or not decisive, is giving great satisfaction to the various classes whose occupations are disturbed thereby. The theatrical companies are, perhaps, the greatest sufferers, for these contests afford an amount of comedy such as is rarely seen behind tho footlights. In Manches- ter, for example, Mr. Balfour, on the one hand, and Mr. Birrell, on the other, have been entertain- ing audiences with their smart responses to the questions with which they have been heckled. At Oldham Mr. Winston Churchill has amused the operators with his epigrams, his definition of a candidate being the most whimsical—as "a person who is invited to stand when he wants a seat, and rs expected to lie." The rotten egg form of criticism has given place to the cartoon, and in many dis- tricts peculiarities have been hit off with a pretty wit. It is, however, in the house to house canvassing that the most curious Contretemps occur. That is a well reviued story now applied to Battersea, which tell of the disap- pointment of one party worker who, thinking that he was on the track of a supporter, was informed at the cottage that the elector had died—or,as the widow put it, had "gone to Heaven." "I am sorry to hear that," said the canvasser absent- mindedly. Mr. Bottomley's rejoiner to a beery in- dividual who asked rather sharply whether it was his desire to put down the drink traffic, was hardly less opportune: "I am sorry that the law will not allow me to ask you all to help me do so." It was a young Conservative in the 1892 election who made the best answer at the gibe about his youth: "Does sour mother know you are out ?" "Yes, and next Friday (polling day) she'll know I am in." These pleasantries relieve politics of its sharpness and passion.

"To be forwarned is to be forearmed," and we are glad to note that the warning to the country from the outbreak of the dreaded bubonic plague an Glasgow has not passed unheeded in Warring- ton. Dr J Guest Gornall has presented & memor- andum on the subject to the Medical Officer of health, and which the latter has wisely had included in the agenda of last Tuesday's Council meeting. Dr Gornall's research has given him a valuable knowledge of plague forms and symptoms and this knowledge he devotes to the safeguarding of the public. He po;nts out the ease with which the fell disease may be disseminated and the great possibility of its being brought to Warrington either via the Ship Canal or by rail. Mildly-infected persons would be able to travel and pursue their ordinary avocations, spreading the plague as they went, so that it is as well—nay imperative—that we in Warrington should be fully prepared to cope with the plague should it unfortunately be introduced in our midst. Dr. Gornall's plan of action in the emergency is excellent, and should ensure the dreaded plageo a brief existence in the neighbour- hood. We hope the thanks of the Council, on behalf of the townspeople, will be given to Dr. Gornall for his valuable suggestions.

There are no doubt hundreds of people in the town who are ignorant of the fact that Warrington has, once, been visited by the plague, and they may be excused for their ignorance by the fact that the case occurred so long ago as 1647. In Latchford, however, the memory of the plague is a cherished legend, for the outbreak occurred in Wash-lane, and the" plague house" as it is called 18 to be seen yetand frequently forms a subject for the camera and the brush. Dr. Gornall remarks that " A case occurred there about 1647, through infec- tion having been brought from some other locality, and apparently there was no spread beyond the in- mates of the farrn,known to this day as the Plague Etouse (now divided into two cottages), for the People of the neighbourhood kept them strictly in quarantine, bringing them food in exchange for money, which, for safety's sake, was put in vinegar m a hollow on the top of a stone."

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

During the present Parliamentary election it has been discovered by more than one candidate that contesting a seat in the liouse of Commons is a costly luxury. The total expenses in connection With the contest now in progress, it is estimated, Will not be less than three quarters of a million sterling. The four contests. beteen 1885 and 1895 cost more than three millions and a half. Under the Corrupt Practices Act election expenses are ac- cording to a fixed scale, but, notwithstanding this the doors of the British Parliament are practically Closed to a poor man. The latter may have studied Politics all his life,and be ever so anxious to devote Whatever talent he possesses to the be.nefit of his country, but he has no chance of realising his am- bition unless his friends come to his assistance. Wealthy aspirants, even, have been ruined, or crippled for years, in a financial sense, by two or three political contests. Ot course, there is some- thing radically wrong while such is the case, and ,What seems to be really needed is a measure of Legislation which shall have the effect of further reducing the maximum expenditure of candidates, and also make certain expenses now paid by .them charge upon the Imperial Exchequer. This is not merely a working-man's question; since the heavy expenses of electoral contests is an effectual bar- rier to many men of education who would other- /vise place their services at the disposal of the con- stituencies'

A correspondent who is interested in dates and comparisons. makes it out that the late Parliament

;( f

lasted exactly the same period as the Parliament of 1868, in which Mr. Gladstone first appeared as Prime Minister, namely five years, one month, aud sixteen days. Mr. Gladstone's majority was a large one, but that of Lord Salisbury was the largest in the late Parliament since the time immediately following the adoption of the Reform Act of 1832.

When the revenue ieturns for the first quarter of the year were published, it will be remembered, a considerable falling off in the income was shown, and pessimists declared that the prosperity of the nation was on the wane. The figures for the first three months, however, are often deceptive, and cannot be taken as an indication of the future. It is so in the present instance, since the returns for the second quarter of the year showed so marked an advance that for the six months there is actually an increase or £445,000, as compared with the cor- responding period of the previous year. It has to be borne in mind, also, that during this period the war in South Africa has been continued, together with the trouble in China, both of which circum- stances have tended, as a matter of course, to the disorganisation of trade. While rejoicing in evi- dences of continued prosperity, the country takes only for what it is worth the attempt to make poli- tical capital out of it during the election.

A question frequently asked during the Election, and one to which the correct aßswer is rarely'given, even by otherwise well-informed persons, is that of from what time the life of a Parliament dates which is limited to seven years existence ? Under the Statute of George 1., commonly known a; the Sep- tennial Act, Parliament ceases to exist after seven years from the day on which, by writ of summon, it was appointed to meet. So that the life of the Parliament now being elected will begin from November let, the day on which by the writ of sum- mons, it basibaen appointed to meet.

Much amusnnent was caused at a political meet- ing in the Sleaford division by one ot the speakers declaring that he was strongly in favour of its being insisted upon that beer shall be brewed from pure" halt and mops."

Tao Church Congress which has just been held at Newcastle-on-Tyne brings to mind the fact that no fewer than twenty-two of the olcupants of the uresent Bench of Bishops were the nominees of Lord Salisbury. They are the Archbishops of Canter- bury and York,and the Bishops of London, Durham, Winchester, Bangor, Bristol, Carlisle, Chester, Ely, Lichfield, Liverpool, Newcastle, Oxford, Rochester, St. Albans, St. Asaph, St. David's, Sodor and Man, Truro, Wakefield, and Worcester. The survivors of Mr. Gladstone's Bishops are their lordships of Exeter, Lincoln, Liandaff, Manchester, Norwich, Ripon, and Salisbury. The two Bishops nominated by Lord Rosebery during his brief Premiership are the Bishops of Bath and Wells, and of Herefor.i. The doyen of the Bishops is Dr. Ellicott, who was ap- pointed to the See of Gloucester as long since as 1863 by Lord Palmerston.

Harvest, thanksgiving festivals are now in full swing,and do not appear to be prejudiciallyaffected, if one may judge of the results by the amounts of money received at some of them, by the General Election. in many cases, it does not appear to be considered needful to state the object to which the profits are devoted, and this being so, one may per- haps be excused for mentioning what seems to be a very suitable and deserving object. It appears from the annual report of the Royal Agricultural Benevolent Institution, that the Council of that in- atitution have done their best "to meet the existing distress and provide for the .in- creasing number of applications for relief"; but they feel it incumbent on them for a time largely to limit the election of new pen- sioners, unless their appear:for subscriptions is res- ponded to more substantially." The object of the Institution is to provide pensions for bona fide farmers, their wives, widow3,aud unmarried orphan daughters, who through misfortunes in farming are more or less dependent upon charity for support. Thera are now 1,245 pensioners, many of them more than eighty years of age, being maintained at an annual cost of £26 472. It should only be necessary to mention these facts, to ensure attention to a really deserving and very worthy ohjeet, by pro- moters of these services and gatherings who may be well disposed to help.

The present condition of the agricultural labourer, as disclosed by the valuable information which has been gathered on the subject by the Board of Trade, is evidently far different from what it was when Mr Joseph Arch put himself, a quarter of a century ago, at the head of the great movement which cut- mtnated in the famous strike. At that time they were on starvation wages; they had "almost for- gotten the taste of meat ; bread and weak tea, a bit of hard cheese, a bodl ot bad potatoes, and an occa- sional scrap of bacon, with intervals of gruel, and not quite enough of any of these things; old and patched clothes, leaky boots; a cottage in which their family herded together in sleeping rooms such as a sporting nobleman would not have tolerated for his horses." This is the picture drawn by the historian of the condi- tion of the agricultural labourer, not only in the eastern counties, but in many parts of the country, five or six years before he was given the franchise. But, happily, such a description is not applicable to the labourer's condition in even the worst part of the British Isles to-day, although, doubtless, there is considerable room for improvement in some dis- tricts. The Board of Trade report on the average earnings of agricultural labourers in 1893 shows that, as a rule, in districts where the cash wages are low the labourers get more in the shape of payment in kind, or earn extras; and on land where the wages paid are high, the extras are low.

THE NEW AQUARIUM IN THE PARK.

Opening Ceremony.

A large number of the public assembled in the park on Monday afternoon, when Alderman:Bolton. the Chair- man of the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee, performed the interesting ceremony of turnine on the water in the new aquarium which bai been erected near the aviary. There are eleven tanks, in which are several varieties of fish, including common or yellow trout, Loch Levan trout, rainbow trout, roach, common perch, golden and common carp, golden orge, and tench. One of the tanks contains trout which were hatched in the park in April. A considerable improvement has been effected in front of the tanks by the widening and asphalting of the footpath. Amongst those present at the opening ceremony were the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Robert,), Miss Roberts, Aldermen Bolton, Francomb, Fairclough, Hutchinson and Heaton Councillors A Jolley, Tinniou. Peacock, Ellison, Smethurst and John Evans ; Mr. J Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Mr S Wilson (deputy town clerk),Mr J Fairhurst (borough treasurer),Mr T Longdin (borough surveyor),Mr J H Gornall (medical officer) and Mr Deas (water engineer). Alderman BOLTON said it gave him very great plea- sure to turn on the water to the (19W aquarium, and he hoped it would be a source of enjoyment to the people of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) It was something new, and he trusted that educationally it might prove of some little benefit. In future they expected to have other similar attractions in the park. It was the committee's intention to do all they could to enhance the pleasece of visitors to the park. (Applause.) Alderman BOLTON then turned on the water. and the tanks were inspected. The Mayon said that before separating he should like to take the opportunity on their behalf as well as his own to move a very hearty vote of thanks to Alder- man Bolton for his preseece and for opening this very beautiful and attractive exhibit. (Hear, hear.) Ho ' was sure that he and the Park Committee were to be sincerely congratulated upon this recent and attractive addition t the many things which were beautiful in the gardens of the park. He hoped the Committee would be encouraged by the interest shown that afternoon in. making further developments of this character in the park. At the same time they could not allow this opportunity to pass without acknowledging the great ebt, they all owed to te.e Town CI,-.rk for the personal ntereat he had shewe and groat ain 'mint of work he had done in connection with this additional attraction in the !park. (Applause). He cngratulatel the committee, and proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the Chairman. , (Applause) Alderman FRANC3MB, in seconding said the Chairman and the committee deserved their thanks for the interest they took in the park, and everyone was thankful to the Town Clerk for what he had done to make the park attractive. (Applause.) The vote of thanks was heartily carried. Alderman BOLTON acknowledged the vote and said he was pleased that they had extended a portion of their thanks to the Town 'Clerk for the interest be had taken in the park. If it had not been for him there would have been no aquarium. They began with an experi- ment, and had now one of the finest aquariums to be seen in any park in the country. The attractions in the park generally were second to none in Lancashire or anywhere else. (Applause.) The proceedings concluded with applause for the Mayor and Mayoress.

SERIOUS RESULTS OF A RUNAWAY HORSE. About nine o'clock on Tuesday morning, the streets of Runcorn were considerably enlivened. The conveyance belonging to the London and North-Western Railway used in the delivery of parcels throughout the town was standing outside the station, laden and ready to proceed on its rounds. Inside were Mr C Wooley (parcels agent) and his assistant. From some unaccountable reason the horse bolted. Mr Wooley and his assistant were both thrown out and injured severely; then the horse, continuing its wild career,passed down Low- lands-road and Doctor's Bridge at an alarming rate, but was eventually pulled up by Mr Blundell, of York street, who bravely jumped to its head, when near the Church gates. The peculiar feature is that the horse is an unusually quiet one, and having been in the service of the Company for many years, is considered broken to the usual noise of engines and other traffic. Tao conveyance had one shaft broken through its collision with the gate- way of the station. Sr. PAUL'S WESLEY GUILD. RUNCORN NOTES. ---- [By a Correspondent.]

In these vexatious and cmitentious times, the work of a political writer should be much more difficult than in the times of the great calins that fall between one general election and the succeed- ing appeal to the country; but on this occasion I find my task much easier of accomplishment. As the Tories have commenced, so they continue. Their one desire has been to brand Sir John Brunner with the stigma of Pro-Boerism and Little- F,nglandism. But they have failed, and no have to admit they have miserably failed. Indeed, as the result of the " washing " Sir John Brunner stands out clearer to-day than ever before, in the character of a true English patriot and a lover of Queen and country. To the level- headed Englishman—(and Runcorn people especi- ally)—actions speak louder than words. We have it on the authority of one of the Chairmen of Mr. Samson's political meetings ; "That the people of Runcorn subscribed over £ltloo re- wards the relief fund." All honour to them ! But if that is to be the gauge of patriotism, let us en- quire how much Sir J T Brunner has personally contributed; and what his much maligned firm has done? If we did so,wa should learn that the .E 1,600 for the whole of Runcorn—and to which I candidly say Liberals contributed more than one half—is over-topped.

Mr. ImiFon's accusation that "It is mean to make political capital out of personal generosity "cannot be brought against Sir J T Brunner. Mr Sampson's supporters started the campaign, when Sir John was on the Continent, and painted him with the so- called shortcomings of the firm of Brunner, Mond, and Co., regarding the treatment of their working- men. They drew up a scurrilous and damaging indictment, and "meanly "—I use Mr Imison's word—made political capital out of it. Now, I ask,' though I admit " two blacks don't make a white.' if slander is to be made political capital out of ?

FOOTB

Runcorn versus Oldnam at Ituncorn—and no top- hats —is the interesting fixture for Saturday. It was proposed to have given Sir J. T. Brunner, and Mr. C. L. Sampson, an opportunity of exhibiting their powers as footballers, but to that Oldham have objected, and the only struggle that will take place at Irwell Lane, on Saturday will be one for two points. Truly it is a pity that " Philistine "is Bayed the opportunity ot writing more about the " hats," or it may be "coats" would have been his subject on this occasion. But,enough, what are Runcorn going to do with Oldham ? Can they and will they win ? Such are the questions afloat, but the conditions and doings of the two clubs for this semon do not merit anything but a cautious prediction.

Mrs. Stone rendered the solo" Light in Darkness "(Cowen) in a praiseworthy manner, while the choir creditably sang the anthem" While the earth rernaineth " (Maunder). Mr. R Wadsworth presided at the organ. The collections, which were in aid of the church funds, amounted to over £9.

WILFUL DAMAGE AND THEFTS AT RUNCORN.

Two Young Brothers Implicated.

At the Runcorn Sessions. on Monday, before Messrs. F. J. Normsn and S. Taylor, Charles Hulse, aged 16, and Joseph Fluls ~ 11, two brothers, were charged with stealing two brass shaft fittings, value 103., from Messrs King's works, at Clifton, on the 23rd Srptember, the property of William Henry King.—Artnur Lewis, son of Joseph Lewis. of Sutton Weaver, gave evidence that on Sunday the 23rd September, he went to the works at about two o'clock in the afternoon, in company with the two prisoners. The eider prisoner, Charles Hulse, went; into the works and told witness to give warning it anyone Carrie. In a short time prisoner returned with four pieces of brass, which he hid at the back of the hedge near the works. Tney then wenthorne. Witness admitted that he had been to the works with the prisoners before, but they got nothing on that occasion. Subsequently Mrs. Hulse saw witness and told him that he was to be careful what he said about those things, meaning the brass. The prisoner Charles Hulse had called witness bad names for giving information.—Thomas Dodd, manager of works, deposed to visiting the works and finding two lords.; bushes missing from the machinery. Ha subsequently saw the prisoners' parents, and told them tnat if they would bring the brasses back again there would be no bottler about them—Sergeant Ben set stated that he tmok the lad Lewis to Kieg's Works, and on the way he mat the prisoner Charles Hulse. Lewis pointed to Fluke as the one who had been in the works on the previous day, and had brought out two pieces of brass, and put thorn behind the hedge. Wit- ness made a search,but could not find the brass. Witness subsequently brought both prisoners to the Runeorn Police Station where they made statements which were taken down in writing and which the prisoners signed. These statements contained an admission of the theft. The younger boy admitted that his brother had been to the works on several occasions, and taken brass from the engine, and that he bad sold it to a ragman, The prissner Charles Hulse also made a statement, in which he admitted that he had been to the works and got the brass. He gave one of the pieces to Arthur Lewis, and he carried the other. Witness searched the prisoners' home, but could not find I h brass. He had since been to Warrington and visited all the marirse-store dealers, without recovering it. The prisoner Charles Hulse after- wards made a second statement, in which he said that he took the brass and hid it in the hedge 'afar the works, and that subsequently he wont and got it and took it home and put it in the building close to their house. This was the third time he had taken brass from these works.

Charles William Taylor of 14, Milton-;treet, off Sot- land-road, Liverpool,manager for Messrs Bing and Com- pany, gave evidence to the effect that since Easter Monday, twelve months ago, seven bushes of bras; and caps had been removed from their works. The cost of these things and replacing them would he at least ED, fire bushes out of the seven would cost £3 apiece. There had alt.:o been at least 13cwt. of iron shafting removed, Witness had seen the elder prisoner lurking about the premises.—The prisoners now pleaded not guilty, and in reply to the Bench, said their statements made in writ- ing at the Polico Station and signed by them were un- true. Both deniod that they had over been in the works —Soptrintendent Pearson said he had never met two more untruthfol boys in any other case before. lie was afraid they had been encouraged by their parents, and particularly by their mother.—Qiestioned by Mr. N nrnan (the chairman), prisoners said they were telling lies when they admitted they had taken the brass. They were telling the truth when they said they had never been on the premises.—The Bench fined the elder prisoner 203. and costs, or 14 days' hard labour, and ordered the younger prisoner to rtceive six strokes with the birch rod.

NOTES ON CIAMES AND ATHLETICS' Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist."]

Warrington have lost nearly all the points they gained in the opening matches of the season, when they made a clear profit of 5 points on last season's accounts. The successive defeats at S:ockport and Broughton—which were wins last year—havejleft a credit ba,ance of only a single point, so that the high hopes entertained of the team are by no means fulfilled. The last two defeata ought to have been victories if the men bad shaped as they kaow how, but indifferent play allowed the other side to win. Particularly was this the case on Saturday last, when Warrington nad the best of their game with the Rangers, double the chances of scoriug,and yet they lost.

Perhaps the presence of two :would-be members of Parliam;.,nt over-awed the visitors, for certainly a couple of 'em kicking off in one match is rather a big job. The kicks were not worth inw.ta however, and in tact, the kick on behalf of the Warrington team was so poor as to discredit such a fighter as Mr. J. E. Lawton. It gave the Rangers quite an advantage and they never lost it until they got ahead.

Though there was such a big attraction at Swinton the crowd at Broughton was a large one, fully 5,000 spectators lining the arena and filling up the huge stand left over from" Savage South Africa." This is an im- mense strurture and is quite the pride of all Brough- tonians. It is certainly something exceptional ia the way of football field grand-stands. The turf too was brand new—specially laid for the season—and guaran- teed strong as wire. It looked very well too, and the Rangers' executive have to be congratulated on their enterprise. There should be some better foot- ball on such a surface as is now provided. It is not a lawn by any means but as football fields go it will take some beating. Perhaps Wilderspor4l is the only ground that can give points to Wheater's Field even ye:.

A very fast open game was the outcome of the meet- ing of Warrington and the Rangers, and the crowd found plenty of entertainment in the doings of the teams. Warrington b...sean in very promising style, and the way .the forwards heeled out, coupled with the alertness of the backs, made things look lively for the Ringers. Still there was nothing one-sided about the play and on more than one occasion a scere against Warrington was narrowly averted. The latter were the more dangerous however, and Burgess made some wonderful individual efforts which would have resulted in tries had he oassed or kicked at the right time. His selfishness and lack of judgment unfortunately spoiled what was far and away his best display—and moreover it lost Warrington the game. We scored first, after a long attack, Isherwood smartly dropping a goai,and at halfjtinie the score was still two poiats to nil in our favour.

The second half was pretty much tha same except that the home forwards seemed to improve while the Warrington men tired, perceptibly towards the close. From a scrimmage close to the line W James threw wide to Wilson at centre, and the latter transferring to Hogg, he scored at the cnreer. The tactics were very simple but effective, and if triad by Warrington on Fish's wing would have resulted just as favourably to us. The re- verse gave Warringt m renewed vigour and a few minutes later the indefatigable Mereweather pro- fited by Harry's misfielding, and Warrington again led by two points. They seemed to have the game well in band when another determined effort by Broughton drove them elsewhere, and the same long passing being repeated, Isherwood and Fish were caught napping and Wilson scored a good try, Only a few minutes re- mained to play and Warrington tried hard to score again. Broughton's passing was tried, too late, and Fish was hemmed in near the line. The end soon came with Broughton winning by a single point.

Warrington have entirely themselves to blame, and had the halves, particularly Burgess, passed out smartly the result would have been much different. Burgess time after time ran through the opposing halves, and should have at once passed to his centres, but he ran on till he was tackled. Until he can realise that his part of work is done when he has beaten his man, he will never be a good half back, He has lost us the Stockport and Broue,hton matches, and no wonder some of the players are beginning to growl. He has just the one fault, otherwise his play is excellent. Bito and Fish are especi- ally indignant at the way be hugs the ball, and I believe tne Capc,ain's absence was due to no other cause than his inability to make the junior half back play the proper game.

Had the ball been sent along to the backs on Saturday Fish would have snored more than once, but as things went he was terribly neglected and never bad a chance worth going for. Isherwood played much better, Flockenhall being the failure of the three-quarter line. lie lacks th 3 speed and dash necessary co be a try. getter of valne, though, of course, he would do more with better centre play. The centre depends on the half-bicks, and until they feed the men behind War- rington will go on losing matches. Hallam again played well, and was never at fault. The forwards were over- weighted and this tpld in the latter portion ot the gam2.

The Rangers are not a bad team, and have a strong speedy lot of backs, if not very scientific. The two James's give the men behind heaps of chances and they would have to be poor indeed to miss them all. There was as much difference as b2tween chalk and cheese between the style of the two pairs of hatves and the unselfishness of the home couple won them the match when they had less opportunity than their opponents.

(b) That the engineer recommended that the water main in Park-road be continued to meet the main opposite the cemetery, for the purpose of supnlv- ing water to Earlestown, at an estimated cost of £270, the distance being 650 vars. It was resolved that the recommendation of the en- gineer as to the extension of the water main from Park- road to the main Qpposite the cemetery, be adopted, and the work be carried out by the Council. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN• [BY A CORRHSPONDENT.] FOOTBLLL.

The defeat Earlestown sustained on Saturday at the hands of Southport Central did not go down at all well with their supporters. After having vanqished Darwen,and Uhorley,they naturally anti— cipated easily overcoming the Sandgrounders, but their cherished hopes ot another couple of points received a rune shock, the whistle blowing with Earlestown in a minority of a single goal. The winning goal was not obtained until the last few minutes when a draw seemed assured, and the spectators were beginning to leave the ground. Thei point was obtained more by good luck than good judgment as C3ttam had the misfortune to head. through his own goal. However it was no more than the Central deserved for they simply ran away with the homesters,and would have won by a muchi larger majority, had not Chew been in his best humour. He kept goal in splendid fashion saving shot after shot in every conceivable position. The quality of his play can be judged from the fact that for fully a quarter of an hour at a stretch in the first half, the Central were simply shooting in at Earlesto wn.

For to-morrow's match with Ifaydock, the team. has been slightly re-Hganised. Linton takes his place as centre forward again, and Leach re-appears at inside left, whilst the right wing consists of IThitehouse and Bartley. Earlestowu should have) noldifficulty in getting into the second round.

Newton and Warrington must either have beett in a scoring mood or else the defence on both sides was not equal to the attacks of the forwards, no less than six goals being scored. Each side claimed three of these so that the match ended in a draw, Newton, no doubt, being quite satisfied with this, as very few teams will visit,Arpley and come away with points.

The Rovers were without.a fixture and are again resting to-morrow.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPET

HOT-HEADED—A volcano. FERRYBOATS are built for cross purposes. DON'T believe all who agree with you; some people are bored by argument. NONE but the brave deserve the fair, and tiny cannot always support them. ONE half the world doesn't know how the other half lives on its husband's salary. SOME women will use a hammer to drive a tack, but most of them prefer a hair-brush. Soma men smile as though it were a greab favour to the world. As excellent cure for sleeplessness is to run over the names of your creditors. THE only lucky thing there is about the horse- shoe over the door is that it doesn't drop on your head. "IT beats all how some people spend money." " Yes ; and it beats all how some people get money to spend." MOST people are not content merely to draw their own inferences; they have to colour them as well.

EVERY man is fond of striking the nail on the head, but when it happens to be his finger-nail his enthusiasm becomes wild and incoherent. WHEN two brothers marry girls in the same family it is a sign that if there are many more girls in the family, they are worth going after. "HAS your aunt's will been proved yet ?" "No. There is so much trouble about the pesky thing that I almost wish aunt had never died.": WHEN some people fancy they know a thing or two they think they have finished their educa- tion.

A SEA HORSE.—The captain of a vessel wished to purchase a horse, and applied to a dealer, who sold him one. After the purchase had been com- pleted the sailor observed, " Well, now the horse is mine, tell me candidly whether he has any faults, and what they are." "What do you mean to do with him ?" asked the other. " Why to take him to sea," said the captain, "to the West Indies." "Then I will be candid," said the dealer, "he may go very well at sea, but on land he won't go at, all !" As TILE TWIII IS BENT—The inspector, on one of his periodical visits, asked a number of questions of the pupils in a school about the little things in the world. "How many seed compartments are there in an apple?" he asked, and "On which jaw has the cow her teeth ?" with several similar questions. to which the pupils could make no reply. The next day one of the teachers was amused to overhear the following conversation among the pupils in the play-yard. A little girl said, gravely, to some of her companions about her: "Now, children, let us play I am the super- intendent. You've got to know more about common things; if you don't, you will all grow up to be fools. Now tell me," she said, looking sternly at a playmate, "how many feathers has a hen ? "

---- - FARMING IS BEST —An American farmer, who was posses,ed ot some means, entered the office of his local paper, and asked for the editor. The farmer was accompanied by his son, a youth of seventeen years, and as soon as the editor came forward, spoke as follows :—" I came ter git some information." Editor: "Certainly. Be seated." The farmer sat on one end of the table, while his son skit on the floor. "This boy o' mine want ter go into the literary business, an' I thought you'd know ef thar wuz any money in it or not. It's good business, ain't it ?" Editor (hesitatingly) "Why—yes. I've been in it myself for fifteen years, and you see where I've gotto." The farmer eyed him from head to foot, glanced around the poorly-furnished office, surveyed the editor once more, then, turning to his son, who was still sitt- ing on the floor, said :—" Git up, John, an' go home, an' go back ter plowin' ! BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

THE WARRINGTON SHOOTING CASE.-PRISONER DISCHARGED.

A youth named Arthur Jonea, again appeared at the Warrington Polies Court on Monday, on the charge of unlawfully wounding Annie Beatrice Stewart by snoot- ing her with a pistol in Bridge-street, at 8.15 on Thursday evenirm.—The Chief Constable stated that prisoner was a 'bus guard in the employ of the Warring- ton Carriage Gampany and was on duty on the Latch- ford route. At 815 on Thursday night the 'bus made a journey from Latchford to Warrington and girl Stewart was a passenger. Like maus. other little servant girls she kept up a sort of nodding and giggling acquaintance with the guard and they exchanged certain pleasantries. Subsequently the prisoner produced a small pistol and said to the girl "If you say that I'll shoot you," at the same time pointing the pistol at her. There was an ex- plosion and Stewart found she had been shot in the cheek. The prisoner made no attempt to evade the police and when charged said it was a pure accident. Of courie nothing had transpired to prove that it was an accident. The bullet had not yet been extracted but the girl was quite out of danger and out of pain. As it was tin prisoner's first appearance and it was the firs:: criminal case that had come bafore the Bench since Warrington became a County Borough be begged leave to withdraw the case. Prisoner was accordingly dis- charged. A VIOLENT TIIIEF.—Two POLICEMEN SAVAGELY ASSAULTED.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before Mr F Monks and other magistrates.Jobn Broderick.of fown Hill,was charged with stealing from the stall of Rebecca Locker, in the Market Place, at 10 p.m. on Saturday, a tame pigeon of the value of eightpence.—He was further charged with assaulting P.C.'s Leonard and Frank Cooper at the same time.—The Chief Constable said that P C. Leonard noticed the prisoner going about the Market from stall to stall in a suspicious manner. Be kept handling articles whieh he had no in:ention of buy- ing. The officer eventually saw him go to Mrs Locker's stall, take a pigeon out of a crate and walk away. Prisoner was arrested, and he became very violent. Be kicked the constable and made strenuous efforts to es- cape. Leneard sent for assistance and Police Con- stable Cooper came on the scene. He was also kicked about the ribs and legs, and the prisoner said he would '"do twelve months for him." It took the united efforts of thren officers and a civilian to get the man to the Police Station. He was an incorrigible offender, having been before the Bench 15 times. Ho was an inveterate market thief, and had had three months for stealing a till from the market. The Bench committed the prisoner to t aol for a month for the theft, and five months for the assaults.

EXEMPTION FROM VACCIvATION.—John James Hallwood,63, Balfour-road, Runcorn, applied for a certtficate exemptimz his daughter from vaccination. In reply to the Magistrates' Clerk, the applicant said he conscientiously believed that vaccination would be prejudicial to the health of the child. His oldest boy. after he was vaccinated, suffered from eczema, and he had had two cases since. He obtained an exemption certificate for the last child, and it had been the healthiest of the family. The exemption was granted. A SHOP LIFTER 130UND OvEß.—Mary Annie Spruce, aged 17, was charged with stealing a fur, value 23s lid, the property of Thomas Leach, Church-street, Runcorn, on the 29th September.—Dora Stubbs, assistant to the prosecutor, stated that between 12 and 12-30 on Satu:clay prisoner came into the millinery department of Mr. Leach's shop and asked to look at some furs. Witness took four or five furs out of the window and showed them to the prisoner. She (prisoner) then said there was a particular one In the window she would like to look at—a brown one. Witness went to the window

to get two or three more furs, and after examining them prisoner said she had to go to another place with some sage she carried in a basket, and she left the shop. As soon as she had left witness missed the fur produced, the nric, of which was 233 11d. She informed Mr. Leach of what had taken place, and subsequently told P.C. Nicholls. The prisoner was subsequently found, and P.C. Nicholls found she had the fur underneath her cape in a cloth. Prisoner then asked the constable to forgive her, and she would never do it again. She said her father would kill her. The prisoner Was then taken to the police station.—P.O. Nicholls cor- roborated. The prisoner denied that she had the fur three times. She said she found it on the shop floor. When charged she said " I did take it. It's the first thing I ever took."—Mr. Burton, who apreared for the prisoner, said she pleaded guilty, and was sorry for having c,mmitted the offence in a moment of temptation. She assured him it was her intention to have taken the fur back before she met the officer.—Superintendent Pearson said the prisoner had E 1 lie lid upon her when she was arrested. —The Bench bound • the prisoner over to come up for judgment when called upon. ANOTHER ARMY SCANDAL. --- Many Medals but no Pension.

A Hero of Thirty-Five Battles

Tan" Cardiff Times " prints an astonishing example of War Office dispermitioes, and one which ,pparenuy needs looking into. An old soldier of the Mutiny, a veteran of China, India, and Afghanistan. holding no leas than five g .01 coniuct badges, and wearing (says our contemporary), more medals than an.; other man iti Wales, is abeotutely neeiected by an ungrateful country, an I receives no a p:snne f pensioa to his old age. It is admi:ted that this man—James Vett, han—lotn- rnitted in the coivse of his military career one small breach of discipline : but this is, surely, no muse for the callous neglect with which the War Office has treated him. He en lees; no banes ab:m the fault chereei against him—which is certainly no a grave matter. The following is his statement of the case : In the year 1883 I was home on furlough, got mar- ried, and ovemayed my leave. When I returned to re- join my regiment, which I did in the usual way in uniform, a regimental police officer saw me, and know- ing I was overdue took me in charge. For this breach ' of discipline, and the only occasion I teceived any pun- ishment, for I bold five good conduct badges, I had to , forfeit my right t ) a pension. "I havd made repeated applications to the War Office authorities, and amongst otives wrote to the Marquis of Hartington, the then Secretary for War, who as you know is now Duke of Devonshire, in order to get the sentence of the cour--martial nullified, but to no avail. " Yes," continued the voteran, in reply to a question. " I have appliel for the p•snsion given to Indian Mutiny veterans, and as I went through the Indian Mutiny I cannot be deprived of that and lam looking forward to receiving one of these days my 101. a day on this score. In aidition I shall by-and-bye come in for nay old ago pension, to which also I am entitled." "In the meantime, how are things going on with you ? "asked the "Cardiff Times" reporter. "I cannot complain. I am now in the enjoyment of vary good health, and follow my employment regularly as a labourer at the Barry D)cks, but of course I have to take care of my health. Some time ago I found my- self suffering from pains in the back,across the kidneys, ' and in the head. At times I felt as if I could lie down and never get up again; but as I have a wife and family to look after I could not afford to be laid UD. A friend of mine, some two and a half or three years ago, advised me to take Dr.Willianre pink pills for pale peeple. I had tried many remedies before without receiving satisfactory results, hut on my friend's advice I got a box of these pills, and they put me right at once. "This is the only remedy I can get to k ‘op me in a proper state of health. I always keep them in toe house and I believe them to be the very best medicine in the world any Man, woman, or child can take. They have prevented my beine laid up at home, as I should have been had I not the right thing handy. They are splen- did things for the appetite too, for if I c anuot eat a crust one day, after taking a couple of Dr Williams' pink pills I can eat a couple of pounds of dry bread [be next morning." Vaughan. who has fought in thirty-five engagements, lives at present at 27, Hirwain-street, Barry Docks. Be spent twenty-one years and eleven months in India, and in a" Tit Bits " competition for the veterans who had been in the greatest number of battles he tosk a prize. He served under Havelock. Outram. Gordon, and Lord Roberts, and was twenty-six years with the colours, being twice wounded: and his reward from a grateful country is the privilege of earning his own living as best he can, without a penny of help from the nation. It is fortunate that at his age (he is sixty-five) he has been able to maintain his health by the means he describes so candidly : but in this respect he is only one in a mighty army of old men and young men, children, and women of all ages, who have owned their in- debtedness to Dr. Williams' pink pills. They cure the rheumatism and faulty digestion so common in old persons; but they alio) have a sovereign efficacy against lament's, the bane of the young, consumption, sciatica, paralysis, and the disorders which affi ct only women.

At the close of the meeting a hearts, vote of thanks was accorded to the host and hostess, Mr and Mrs Rashwortb, to which the host suitably responded. Several toasts were proposed including " Toe Queen and Royal Family,"" The President," and "The Com- mittee.' Toe proceedings passed very pleasantly. Picnic to Acton Bridge.

As there is no bawling green attachei to the new Midland Hotel, Warrington, Mr- Sim Wakefield, the landlord, decided to take those of his friends fond of the popular summer game and others to the Railway Hotel, Acton; Bridge. Saturday last was the day fixed,and the weather being favourable a pleasant outing was the result. The party,to the number of about 50,journeyed to the hotel in brakes supplied by the Carriage Com- pany, the route taken being through Stretton. Arrived at Acton Bridge, a main of bowies was arranged, and play w,s soon in full swing, the green, although it is a comparatively new one, being in excellent condition. It is most pleasantly situated, and the hotel joins Acton Bridge Station. There was some very good play in one or two cascs, and some exciting finishes. There were 42 entries. Shortness of time, however, prevented the main being brought to a finish. Various prizes were offered. The players and others subsequently sat down toan excellent repast, supplied by Mr John R Reading, the lanalord,and later on the party returned to Warring- ton, all voting that the outing had been a very success- ful and happy one.

Health Committee. Precautions against Plague.

At a meeting of the Health Committee a memoran- dum prepared by the Deputy Medical Officer of Health—Dr. J. Guest Gornall—on the treatment of cases and suspected nisei of buboaic plague was sub- mitted. It stated that the disease might possibly be in- trolucNl in three ways. 1. By Ship Canal men going on b-ard infected vessels, supposing such to have slipped through the sanitary cordon now established at Liverpool and the Etstham end of the Canal—a possible, though perhaps not likely contin- gency. 2 Through sailors from Glasgow or Lascars from the East coming ashore at Latchford locks, as they do sometimes when a vessel is berthed there for the night. 3. Through cases coming overland by road or train from Giagow. This is not at all unlikely, for per one affl cted the milder variety of the disease. The D'puy Medical Officer of Health submitted a plan of action in case the disease got into the town, and alFo sugge-ted that the medical officer should have a freel hand to act without any unnecessary waiting for con- I sultation with the Health Committee.
