The Dredging of the River. Strong Complaints.

The Rivers Committee reported that the following resolution was received from the Warrington Chamber of Commerce :—" That the attention of the Council having been brought by members who are greatly in- terested in the navigation of the Mersey to the fact that the dredging is quite inadequate, and that it is inopos- sible to use the river as intended, until the ship Canal Company comply with their obligations under the Act, the secretary be requested to urge them to give the matter their serious attention forthwith." The Rivers Commi tee resolved that the Town Clerk inform the secretary of the Chamber of Commerce that the subject matter of the resolution has had and is having the serious attention of the cemmittee and that they lave that the steps they are taking in the matter will lead to a practical result. The MaYou,in moving the adoption of the minutes,said he could only corroborate the nature of the Rivers Committee's reply to the Chamber of Commerce. At the same time be would like, if it were possible to emphasise to the fullest possible extent the tact that the river at the present time was in an abominable and disgraceful condition. The Ship Canal Company were not making any serious attempt to get the river to that depth and width of channel which their obligations imposed upon them. He was disgusted with the whole business and he was quite certain that the Corporation might have put themselv -co into the position of forcing the com- pany's hands if they had cared to do 93. The Corpora- tion were fully determined to take the matter up and be hoped they would be "backed" up not only by the traders of the town but by the townspeople also. If this river were allowed to get into such a condition as to make it umeless for tradieg purposes, the industries in Warrington would suffer to an enormous extent. He had had occasion to write to Mr. Hunter on the matter

that day. Vessels drawing six feet had been ucable to get from Bank Quay to Walton Lock, with the result that the cargoes had to be lightered down the river to the vessel. In face mf the fact that the Ship Canal Com- pany's obligations required them to make a channel of eight feet, such a condition of affairs as he had referred to ought not to be allowed a moment longer than was absolutely necessary. Alderman HEATON said he endorsed what the Mayor had said with reference to the matter. The Corporation had given the Ship Canal Company every opportunity of carrying out their obligations. There had been no improvement in the condition of the river, in fact, it was going from bad to worse, and the time bad arrived when the committee should be supported in compelling the company t 3 carry out their agreement. Councillor TINNION said he was exceedingly pleased . .

that the Rivers Committee was taking uto,the -matter with energy. He hoped something would be done before long to remedy the present disgraceful state of the river. The river between Bank Quay and Walton Arcla,s bad got into such a state owing to continued neg- lect, that when the company brought their vessel which towed the hoppers, it actually stuck in the bed of the river, and it was only drawing about four feet of water at the time. When they found the river in a condition like that il was time that energetic measures were taken to have it remedied. _ _

d-o-nn-cillor BURTON said he also waq very pleased that

the matter was being taken up. The river was in a dis- graceful state at present, and would not be navigable at all unless a serious attempt was made to 'lre Igo it. He had hoard that the C3mpany had let the contract for dredging to someone els3,but that could not relieve them of their respon9ibility in the matter, Councillor BENNETT asked when thjtime of the Corpora- tion's agreement with the ship Canal Conapany to con-

_ atruct docks at Warrington expired? The M&YOR thought that Councillor Bennett ought to have given notice of such a question as that. The Cor- poration were allowed ten years from 1896. Alderman SUTTON said it was quite evident to him that if they intended to p.rsist in the Ship Canal Com- pany carrying out their obligations they must take some definite steps at once. It would not do to let the thing go on, because if they did, they might depend upon it that the company would consider there was no necessity for noticing any of their complaints and ultimately they would find that the dredging of the river would be utterly neglected. He therefore thought it was time they came to some definite understanding with the company and, if necessary, to force them to take such a course as to prevent tl'e river from again getting into such a conclitkn as it was in at the present time. Let the Corporation have it settled once for all, or his firm conviction was that the company would endeavour to gat out of their obligations and take no interest in the matter whatever. He approved of what the RiverS Committee had done. The minutes were then pasced. The Water Extensions.

The Proposed Taxation of Ground Values The following communication on the taxation of ground values was ordered to be entered on the min- utes :—Town Clerk's Office, Accrington, 190 a Septem- ber, 1900. Dear Sir. Taxation of ground values. In ucordance with instructions therein contained, I beg to enclose you copy of resolution adopted by the Town Council of this Borough on the 3rd inst.—Yours faith- fully, A. 11. AITKEN, Town Clerk. Resolved "that in the opinion of this Council, the present system of local taxation is unjust, because it exempts the owners of land within the municipal boundaries. That,at present, the incidence of the local rates operates as a tax upon industry, enterprise, and thrift, and constitut2s a griev- ance of which all occupiers of urban property are justi- fied in demanding immediate redress. That, in the opinion of this Council, the continuous increment in the value of urban land is not the result of any exertion on the pirt of the landlord, but is due to the collective industry and enterprise of our townspeople, and is there- fore a peculiarly suitable subject for taxation. That it is necessary that a separate valuation be made of sites as distinct from buildings or improvements, and a rate made and levied so as to fall on the owners of the land values thus assessed ; and that all land, whether occu- pied or unoccupied, be taxed according to its value. Further, that copies of this resolution—representing the opinion of this Council on a public matter of the ut- most importance—be forwarded to all assessing autho- rities in Great Britain.'

VALOUR OR BRAVADO.

600 Nowadays we keep hearing of " most disastr- f chances—of moving accidents by flood and hair-breadth' scapes in the imminent deadly breach, being taken by the insolent foe," and we, like Des Air mona and her father, " devour up our soldiers' "40 course." But is there to be no distinction beo,ll„en-90 honest L'valour and Ihare-brained foolhardiness? who find a bubble reputation at an infinite, yet ."-eeC warrantable risk, may have their actions applauded. hie very seldom approved. THE EXAMINER, RUDA Y, OCTOBER, 5, 1906

===== 0002601_19001005_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED

SIMULTANEOUS MISSION,

Free Churchmen in

Conference at Warrington,

===== 0002601_19001005_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
A "Daring" Idea.

A simultaneous mission conducted by the united Free Churches throughout the country is proposed to be held in the opening months of 1901. To discuss the matter, meetings in connection with the West Lancashire and West Cheshire Federation of Free Cnurch Councils, were held in Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom last Thursday. The proceedings commenced with a conference of dele- gates and friends which was held at 2.30 in the after- noon. The Rev C F Aked, of Liverpool, occupied the chair, and amongst those present were the Revs Robert Veitch, Liverpool ; C E Hocken,J Yonge, J F Matthews. E T Reed and C Finch, Warrington, The Rev Thomas Law, secretary of the National Council, was announced to speak but was unable to be present. The Rev. C. F. AWED said•they had met by the ccur- tesy of the Warrington Council for the purpose of taking counsel as to the proposed great simultaneous mission at the beginning of the coming year. After explaining t oat the Rev T Law was unable to attend that afternoon the rev. gentleman referred to the responsibility of the movement. Unless they could realize the tremendous importance of it and could develop within themselves and within their neighbours, friends and colleagues, a genuine enthusiasm for the work, all the suggestions and examples in the world would not be of much valise to them. There was another movement on foot in the Free Churches of a different character which was also intended to welcome the incoming century. It was now proposed to hold a temperance mission, the object of which was to secure one million new pledges during the first year of the century. Now they could see for themselves how, if the mission were a success, it would be a help to every form of temperance propaganda ; and every form of social activity. A million pledges would at least repre- sent a very great part of the work done--work for which temperance organisations existed. They would represent the equipping of every branch of the temperance movement, and a reinforce- ment of vigour, by which all temperance organis- tions would be enormously helped. Lift that on a still higher platform and consider that, if their ; dream were realized,they would secure a million conver- sions to Christ, a million actual decisions for the Lord Jesus. It would demonstrate to the world ia a way I absolutely beyond their dreams, their unity and their strength,—their unity for a great effort, their strength for carrying out that effort. It was the very bigness of the movement, the seeming teoer impossibility of it, which comoituted its charm and attraction. If the only objection was its impossibility they had better get to work at once. It was much too vast, and it was far too big for them to accomplish but that was precisely the charm which would attract them. Referring to the mission under consideration, the speaker said that day by day the same gospel would be preached throughout the country, the same appeal would be made and the same prayers would ascend to the throne of God. That demonstration of unity they were intending to make would be of value for years to come. There never had been any dissension in the ranks of the National Free Church Council between thsse whose work had been largely social work and those who had given themselves more to those forms of work which thoy called spiritual. It had been recognised that both kinds of work were needed and neither would win its largest fruition unless both prospered. They were united now as they had been united from the first. (Applause.) The Rev, R YEITCH then delivered a brief address. He sad the movement was a magnificent idea,a kir,d of inspiration, and it would join them together in one great religious revival or mission in which to initiate the next century. Whensoever that idea dawned on any manitio believed it was truly a thought given from God, It was a very daring suggestion to dream of bringing the whole of the Free Churches of this land into one great movement and yet they were engaged to undertake the task. On looking back on the past history they found that there was a great revival in the latter part of the 18th century. At the present time there were far greater advantages for such a movement than existed in the eighteenth century. Was it net possible with resources at their disposal that a mission might be initiated which would make the twentieth century stand on a higherlevel than it would have done otherwise.lt seemed to him that there was a pressing need for the mission. They wished to cleanse the land of imparities and they could at least see a dawning hope of the brightness and betterment of life. The desire bad been to cover the whole country. They wanted a revival of religion that might be wo:thy of the name of national, and it was proposed not only to visit the cities and towns but also the villages. (Applause). A discussion on the scheme followed, and members of the Conference reported as to arrangements made in the districts they represented. Tbe Rev. JoHer YONGE said that in Warrington the matter had been seriously considered for some time, and they were hoping to be able to see their way to arrange a very successful mission at the time arranged. He could net. however, give any details as yet.

THE EVENING MEETING. SOME STATISTICS ON CHURCH-GOING.

There was a good a.Aendance in the schoolroom in the evening when a public demon6tration was held. The Rev John Yonge presided. The Rev R VEITCH read a telegram shich he had received from the Rev Thomas Law regretting his ina- bility to be present and encouraging the Federation to go ahead with the work of arranging the miscion. The CHAIRMAN, in the course :,of his remarks, said that be should like, with their permission, to occupy a few minutes in explainiog ono very powerful reason which impressed his mind very strongly that the ' churches in Warrington should address themselves to the carrying out of the mission in this town. None of them, he thought, would at all question the need , which existed in this town, as throughout the country, he supposed, for a great, deep, and powerful revival of a true and undefiled religion. They all deplored, he was sure, the very large amount of religious indifference which prevailed, not only among those classes tbey were apt to look upon as ungodly and irre- ligious altagether, but unhappily in the ranks of their churches. This must be removed and done away with if the churches of the country were to enter on the new century in anything like the state which became them. Tuley had, of course, to face here in War- rington, the groat unmistakeable face that in these days of ours the estimate of the duty of public worship had most seriously and steadily declined. Some time ago i n this town,so long ago as 1881,one of the local newspapers instituted a census of the attendance at religious worship on the first Sunday in November that and he had before him some of the statit- tics gathered as the result of that inquiry. Before he gave these statistics he would make an explanatory reference to the census which was taken by Mr. Horace Mann in 1851. That very intelligent staid- cian laid down as a reasonable thing that certain allow- ances were necessary for persons, who through unavoid- able reasons were absent from public worship. He este. mated that this would account for the absence of 42 per cent of the population of the country lesyrug 58 per cent as passiole attenders. Comiag to the year 1881. in Warrington they had a population of 41,700. There were then in this borough thirty five places of worship which gave accommodation :for 15,700 persons. On Sunday me Ch of November, the gross attendance at the morning, afternoon and evening ser- vices,in these thirty-five places was 14,800 persons. Some of course in that number bed been counted twice and perhaps three times so it was considered a fair estimate to allow one fourth of the 14,000 for those attending more than once. Therefore they had to take 3.700 from the estimate, and it was found that only 11,100 attended public worship, a little over 26 per cent. l'here ought to have been according to Mr Mana's estimate 13,000 people mote. In March, 1851, the Government census brought out the result that there was a population in Warringtonof 22,800 and fifteen lacel of worship. The accommodation only provided for 9,700, and it was found that morning, afternoon and evening services were attended by 13,522 nersons ; or 44 per cent of the population, whereas in 1881, there were only 26 per cent. a decrease of 18 per cent. whilst the population! had increased. From the municipal returns, the population of Warrington was now estimated to be 64,000 and since 1881 about thir- teen new places of worship had been added. There was an increase in population of 25,000, had they a corree- pending increase in the numbers attending nublic wor- ship ? He doubted it and it was a terrible thought for them to face. What they deplored in Warring- ton was true probably in all other parts of the country but two blacks did not make a white. It appeared to him to be a most profsundiy sorrowful thing, and it should weigh heavily oa the hearts of Christian people. Was not this reason enough for a mission ? In conclusion he hoped that by the mission they would bring about a more earnest care for the things of God and a deeper spiritual life in the people. (Applause.) The Rev.R.Lswis WARBURTON addressed the meeting. He remarked that they were in the midst of dangers greater than England had ever faced before, for be be- lieved the powers that opposed righteousness had grown and would grow until the ler great conflict. Before the new century dawned he wanted them to consider what they could do in the century that was to come. They had opportunities which their forefathers did not possess and by the mission they hoped to call the people to a saner life, and to a higher religion to make a holier nation and a nobler citizenship. (Applause.) The Rev W TOWNEND also made a short speech and explained what arrangements had been made, up to the present. He thought that all Free Churches should be united and he had never seen the reason for twenty six different Methodist denominations. He looked upon the Free Church Council as the greatest movement of the century.

_ Toe usual votes of thanks concluded the meeting.

SESSIONS.

Lipton's Refused a Licence.

On Friday morning, the Mayor (Alderman Roberts) pre sided over a full bench of Magistrates at the Borough Police Court, when the adjourned licensing sessions were held.

Lipton's Secretary as a Shopkeeper. Wine Licence Refused.

Mr. Forshaw applied on behalf of William Shaw Carmi- chael, the Secretary of Messrs. Lipton's, Limited, for the renewal of a Wine and Spirit Licence for the shop, Priory- buildings, Bridge-street, Mr. Tobin. Liverpool, opposed the application on behalf of the police. Mr. Forshaw said the licence had been granted b3for eln the name of the Secretary of Lipton's Limited, lie now asked for the renewal of the licence. The company held licences in several Lancashire towns, and the licences were in the name of the secretary of the company. That being so be thought the magistrates would agree that the secretary was a proper person to hold the licence. There was no case upon the point, but in the case of Regina v. Lyon. Lord Justice Smith stated that he thought a secretary zooid apply for a licence on behalf of a limited company. Lord Justice Chitty concurred. Mr. Tobin observed that Lord Justics Chitty left the point over for the magistrates. Mr. Forshaw : You have granted this licence to the secretary before. The Mayor said the question upon which Mr Forshaw had to convince them was that the applicant WAS the keeper of a shop within the meaning of the Act of Parliament. The magistrates present disposition was that the keeper of a shop is entitled to have a licence granted to him. Councillor Smethurst: What has he done to have this opposition ? The Mayor said the question was what had he not done? The ground of objection was that he WAS not the keeper of the shop. Mr Forshaw : The company are the owners of the shops, the secretary of the company keeps them. The Mayor: Is any person other than the responsible occupant of the shop the keeper of the shop? The qoestion is one of co-nmon sense. Is the secretary of bigornpany a shopk,eper within the meaning of the Act of Pamerat ? ale. Monks : This is introducing an entirely new principle. If this is granted we may have drinking shops all over the place. William Shaw Carmichael, the secretary of the company, was called, an said be held the licencs for the shop in Bridge-street. He was in the positioa of a private indivi wei who had a dozen shops. The Mayor: We want evidence that you are the keeper of this shop. Witness produced a minute resarding a resolution of the directors of the company empowering him to appl y for licences all over the country. Mr. Monks said it was not a reasonable thing, and did not come within the meaning of the Act. Mr. Forshaw : I submit that it does. Yan have granted it. Mr. Tobin (to applicant): How many licenoes like this do you hold ? ' Applicant: Over 230. Mr. Tobin You recognize that you are responsible for the good conduct of these snOps ?

Applicant —I arts Mr. Tobin : How ean you be responsible for more than 2CO shops in the provinces when you reside in London. Applicant: I have a system of inspection by which War- rington. as well as other places, is visited by a different inspector every week. Mr. Tobin : You recognize that you are personally respon- sible. How many times have you been to Warrington in the last year ? Applicant : Once in the last six months. The Mayor said the mazistrates had made up their minds. With one exception they unanimously refused to grant the application. Mr. Forshaw said the ease would be taken to the Court of Appeal. He would like the magistrates to say upon what grounds they ref u red the application. The Mayor: The applicant is not qualified according to the Act. _

On Mr. Tobin's suggestion the ground of the refusal was stated to be tbat the applicant was not qualified because he did not keep a shop,

The Long Pull.

The Mayor said that with regard to the remarks made at the opening sessions concerning the question of the long pull, some inquiries had been made as to what was meant by them aski g that long pull should be discontinued. He could only say that what was meant was that anything over or less thin imperial measure should be entirely discontinued. Mr. Browne said he was going to ask a question upon this subject. The matter was now quite clear.

The Proposed Rebuilding of the Wheat Sheaf.

Mr Browne, on behalf of Mesirs Greeneii, Whitley and Company made an A ppl i cation for permission to rebuild the "Wheat Sheaf" Inn, off I-inners Brow. They had bought two cottages behind the public house at reason- able prices but th other two were out of the question. Mr. Monks;: You talk abont impossibility. We had a case before that was impossible but the magistrates solved it. Mr. Browne: When I used the word impossible I did not perhaps use the exact term The price is more than any sane person would give for two little houses like that. Continuing Mr. Browne said his proposal was to go aocordinz to the plane submitted, with one exception and that was to make the rooms loftier and to °lnvert the adjoining shop into a pri- vate dwelling house for the licensee. 't here was at present only one wretched kitchen. for the family to live in, and it was also used by customers. The Mayor (to Mr. Browne) ; We will give you a month to turn round in. _ _

Mr. Browne We are not going to be blackmailed. Trio Mayor said if this applioatiou were granted the value of the property would be incre teed enormously. Provided there was a considemtian of a public oho:actor they would listen to whatever application It was proposed to make. Mr. Monks: We are open to any improvement. Mr. Chandley did not agree with the view taken by the Mayor The Mayor said he represented the majority of the justices who visited the spot. He personaily was extremely reluc- tant, but would go with the majority of the magistrates pro- vided there was same consideration of a public character. The magistrates w.ruld either give a decision now upon the merits of the application or al:ow it to be adjourned for a month.

The Bench deeided to adjourn the applization for a month. Mr Chandley:said he aid not think the town had any busi- ness to expect something in the nature of street improvment for the granting of an application of that kind. If the pi..ce was insanitary the Corporation ought to be glad that the work was being done. The application ought to be dealt with on its merits even if it were good or bad. Daring last year the small balance of preference capital was issued amounting to £32,300,m0re for the purpose of com- pleting the amount of stock and making the even sum of £.250,000 Preference Stock thau for any- pecuniary nec.N3sity. and they would find on the balance sheet now that the capital account stood this year at £860,000 instead of as in previous years £827,700. This was the only change that had ever taken place in the capital account of thee Com- pany. There never had been and there was not now any debenture stock or charge of that sort upun the Company's accounts. They stood in the position that they could not replace their ironworks or collieries even if they had nothing to pay for leases or goodwill with any- thing like tne amount of capital they had issued, and he thought they might all be justly proud of having brought the Company to this position. This year for the first time for many years the colliery bad produced the larger amount of profit, the pro- portions contributed being about three-fifths colliery and two-fifths ironworks. There had been no doubt about the good trade at the colliery and also,though a little more fitful,in the iron trade. The total wages paid by the Company during the year amounted to £385,000 as compared with £206,000 in 1874, and £275,000 in 1880. The wages would be again higher this year, and whilst they undoubtedly represented more work they also represented a very considerable addition to the individual earnings of each wage-aarner, and ought to add largely to the comfort and well-being of those in the euiploy of the Company. He was sorry, however, to see that high wages did not mean increased output, so far as coal was concerned ; the total output of this year, notwithstanding every effort of the managers, not being quite equal to that of the previous year. The total sales of the Company, coal, iron and engineering, amounted to .C 1,454,000 and a consideration of this figure would show them how they required capital to do their trade and where so much of their money went to, in book debts and stocks. He should like to add one word of tribute to their Managing Directors at both the iron and coal departments, and to their deputies, for the energy, ability, and care with which they had handled these vast sums for the benefit of the Company.

GENERAL ELECTION

WARRINGTON. Result Of the Poll.

Thursday was polling day at Warrington, the candidates being Mr. A. H. Crosfield (L), and Mr. Robert Pisrppint (C). During the whole of the morning wretched weather prevailed, rain falling incessantly. Consequently some difficulty was experienced in getting voters to the polling booths. At noon, however, a large number of workingmen recorded their votes. In the after- noon the rain ceased, and the polling continued more briskly until eight o'clock in the evening, when the ballot boxes were conveyed to the Town Hall. Shortly before ten o'clock the result was de- clared by the Mayor as follows :

R. Pierpoint 4168 A H Croefield(L).—.—.—.—.—.—... ...—... 3303

Conservative Majority 1165 LkEIT ELECTION (1895) : R. Pierpoint (C), 4,0011; P. B. Scott (14, 3,326 ;—Majority 675.

RUNCORN.

SIR J. T. BRUNNER'S CANDIDATURE.

Great Meeting at Runcorn. COMPLETE REP-El 7 TO SAMSON.

Winnington Workmen Vindicate their Employers. The Real Issues of the Contest.

The Public Hall Runcorn on Saturday night was crowded with an enthusiastic gathering of the sup- porters of Eir J. T. Brunner's candidature. The chair was occupied by Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., who was supported on the platform by Sir J. T. Brunner, Bart., Dr. Bowman, F C.S., Messrs. A. R. Norman, C.C., J. J. Howitt, J. W. Deakin. J. R. Frith, John . Wilson. J. Turnbull, E. Marshall, G S. Frith, J. M. Frith, W. Pendlebury. W. Millington, W. Handley, fl W Handley and others. When Mr. Taylor took the chair Sir John Brun- ner had not put in an appearance, and, when he did so a little later there was a tremendous out- burst of enthusiasm. The vast audience rose en masse cheering wildly and waving hats and hand- kerchiefs, and even ladies moved by the wave of excitement took part in the outburst. It was some minutes before the meeting could be pro- ceeded with. From this point onwards the meeting was characterised by genuine enthusiasm. No point for nor against was allowed to escape without re- cognition, and the few Tories who were present and tried to make a diversion in favour of Mr. Samson's candidature, by their half-hearted criesof "Pro-Boor," were swamped by furores of cheering as sincere as they were spontaneous. In the course of his address Mr. Taylor said ; Sir John Brunner had come before them once again for the purpose of soliciting their support of his candidature. One would have thought after the defeat of the Tories five years ago they would not have had the courage and pluck to come forward again. They had gone through the whole of Cheshire trying to find a Cheshire candidate. (Rear. hear.) But there was not a Cheshire gentleman willing to face the ordeal, for they knew perfectly well what the result would be. (Applause.) In their rounds, however, they had come across a gentleman to fight the constituency;but he Mr Taylor venturedto , say there were not ten people in Runcorn that knew or had ever heard of the Tory candidate before. (Applauee j They had in the days before the fran- chise, had Tory representatives in the division. It always used to be Egerton, Leigh, and Warburton ' —(laughter)—or a variation of those three names. But they never saw those gentlemen only on the occasion of elections, and occasionally when they got a " walk over" he ventured to say they never saw them at all. (Applause.) It used to be the boast of those people that Runcorn supported them, but when the workingmen got their votes and the flit) franchise, it was the last they saw of Egerton. Wf_irburton and Co. ai!'9r andZNlall On the other band Sir J. T. Brunner had serve for something like 15 yam (Hear, heat) it -vedia be a rade a time to recount all that Sir Jehn had done ; but he Would say, that in every good work that had tended to the benefit of the people of the Division; and of the whole of Ches- hire; as well as other places. Sir John had always taken part—(applause)—and he was that day one of the most prominent men in the House of Com- mons. (Applause.) What had Sir John done for Rurcorn 3—[A. Voice: What has he done?] Ur. Taylor: I will tell you—(hear, hear)—and will be obliged to you if you will come forward and can contradict all that I say he has done for Runcorn. (Cheers.) In the present day they were all educa- tionalists; and they knew what a strong educa- tionalist Sir John was, (Applause.) Mr. Taylor then, amidst most enthusiastic outbursts of ap• plause. recounted all that Sir John Brunner had Ilene for the School Board in the matter of the Granville-street School, and for the Technical In- stitute. Sir John Brunner was greeted with a storm of cheer- ing ana " He's a jolly good fellow " on rising, atri it was several minutes before he could pr-mod with his speech. He said he bad been asked to tell over again the po.ition his firm bad taken for years with regard to the workmen. There was no better set of fellows work- ing for any employer from John o' Groats to Land's End than those who worked for him. (Applause.) He bh- lieved the reason of that was that he had always ben kindly disposed towards them and they returnPd that kindly feeling towards him. He started in 1873 with his partner Dr. Mond having saved £BDO out of his wages at Widoes and having a promise from his father and his mother-in-law of money on loan to start in busi- ness and be found when he went lot, the Salt District, where he was bound to go in order to get brine that the wages there were very low. Wages bad gone uo a good deal more than half since then. (Applause.) He found in one di,trict it was impossible to get a freehold and he would not put a valuable works on anything short of a freehold and that gave him a lesson which ought to be a lesson to them that until they amendcd the land laws and enabled every intending manufacturer, no matter what his business might be, to obtain a freehold wher- ever he settled, they would never have full prosperity for this country and this empire. (Hear, hear.) There was no country in the world where the land laws told so heavily against business aa in England. Treatment of Workmen.

The firm was started in October 1873 and from that until October 1879 the market price of alkali fell, and then stopped and Dr. Mond and he agreed that it was time they did something for the benefit of their men. They resolved in the first instance, to give a week's holiday and.a week'a pay with it to every man who was Ftoady. (Applause.) They were now tc-Elay spending at Winnington alone, leaving oat the other works, between £5,000 and £6,000 a year in giving this holiday and pay. In 1889 the firm's progperity was so great that they decided to further improve the condition of the men by changing the divisien of the day from two shifts of 12 hours to three shifts of eight hours. Twelve hours a day for seven days was a dog's life. There had been talk of a compulsory eight hours day f )1. miners but most miners only worked five and six days and SO7lO only 4i days, and in his opinion if they were to legislate for an absolutely eight hours day, let them begin with the men who were bound to work seven days a week for their case was much harder than the miners who only worked five days a week. (Hear, hear.) The firm looked forward-to -iteravy- lose from this change of two shifts to three shifts, and they asked the men to suffer a reduction of ten per cent. from their wages. At the end of a year they made fa calculation and found that although the cost of the change had been consider- able it was much less than they expected, and they gave back to the mea the ten per cent. reduction (Applause). So since 1890 their men got paid exactly the same wage for eight hours as they did formerly for twelve burs. (Applause). In the year 1895 they put all th-ir thy ars at Winnington on what was care I the 48 hours week. Sir.—ln a Liverpool contemporary there is a letter from a shareholder "that refers to a circu- lar accompanying Sir John Brunner's election address, sent by Mr. Brock, the secretary of Brunner, Mond, & CD., disclaiming the statement made by Mr. Sampson the Unionist candidate and his supporters that the firm "Never took on new workmen over 30 years of age." They could not take on new hands over 30 years of age." The Combination.

WARRINGTON V. NEWTON.—&t Warrington on Satur- day. Several alterations were made in the home team, and Newton brought their strongest possible eleven. Mr Robert Pierpoint, the Conservative candidate for War- rington, kicked off amid much enthusiasm,and play was immediately taken to the visitors' quarters. Flannigan relieved, and Hill and Warren, the visitors' right wing immediately became dangerous. Warrington were penalized, but Cook managed to save. Some clever for. ward play by Warrington followed, and Rowbottom centring, Riley had no difficulty in beating Horro7.ks. Warrington continued to attack, and from a forward movement on the left Flannigan nearly let down his side by a miskick. Momentary relief was afforded hy Harrison, and Butler made a feeble attempt to, score,but his shot went wide. Broster and Ellis were conspicuous on the Warrington left, and the former narrowly missed augmenting the score with a strong cross shot. Hill robbed Riley, but again Warrington left were seen to advantage, and some pretty passing between Broster, Murray, and Stewart ended in the latter scoring the second goal for the home team. The visitors were fre- quently pulled up for fouls. Kenny and Butler trans- ferred play into the home quarters, but their shooting was very erratic. The visitors took the ball into War- rington territory, and from Dilworth's miskick Kenny scored. Half time :—Warrington, two; Newton. one. During the interval. Mr. A. H. Crosfield visited the ground in company with some of his supporters. After the restart Newton early on equalised. and play after- wards became of an even nature. Near the close War- rington became very aggressive, and efforts by Riley and Stewart were only made futile by the striking work of the visiting backs. A minute from the close of the game Stewart scored from close ranee amidst much ex- citement. Newton retaliated, and starting from centre tick the ball through the Warrington backs and scored with a long oblique :shot. The whistle then sounded with the score three goals each.

LANCASHIRE LEAGUE. Earlestown v Southport Central.

After an abcence of a fortnight Earlestown turned out on the Mesnes enclosure on Saturday to receive the annual League v;sit of Southport Central. Last saason the Central were soundly thrashed when they played the corresponding fixture, and they there- tore ovidently had this in their minds' eye on Saturday, for from the beginning to the end of the game they were far and away the better lot and with a little luck, their victory of one to nil might easily have been greatly increased. However the goal which by the way was not scored until the last few minutes had been entered upon—was sufficient to ensure them the coveted two points. Up to Saturday the re- cords of the two clubs were very similar, each having 'won two and lost one although the Sandgrounders had ' slightly the better goal average. The Earlestown team had undergone two or three important changes, the recent exhibitions of some of the players not having given satisfaction. The goal ke per and backs were the same as usual but in the intermediate line, Clibborn played vice Whitehouse, who was moved to inside left, whist Bartley wa4 shifted to centre forward. Bell being found a place at inside right, owing to Leach being unable to play. The Central were without Leadbetter, their centre half, who has signed on for West Bromwich Albion and Atherton. their fast outside right; but Kay, who took Atherton's place played a splendid game throughout, and even if the latter player had been on, the wing play could hardly have been better, The teams will how- ever he better understood by the following list:—Earlestown : Chow, goal ; Hey es and Cottam. backs; Pennington, Green, Clibborn, half backs ; Morgan, Bell, Bartley. Whitehouse and Settle, forwards. Southport Central, Rimrner, goal ; Spark and Smith, hacks ; Sinclair, Tasker, and Tati-ertsall half-backs ; Kay Shadboult, Lang, Watson and Halsall, forwards. The attendance wag not very large at the commencement, only about 500 spectators being present whcn Bartley set the ball in motion on behalf of the homesters against a strong wind, but with the hill in their favour. EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

POLICE CASES

At the Earlestown Police Station, on Monday morning, before Mr. Collingwood, James Edgeley, was charged with being drunk on Saturday and was tined 5s and cots.— kdam Hart, of Haydock, for a similar offence was fined 28 61 and costs. ST. JOHN'S YOUNG MEN'S CLASS.

In connection with this class a social gathering was held in the Mission Hall, Earlestown,on Satur- day evening. There were about 90 persons present, and an excellent tea was partaken of, the tables being presided over by Mrs G Dale, Mrs R Thorne, Mrs Rothwell, Misses Spencer, Boon and Sutton. Alter tea,games and dancing were indulged in, the music being supplied by the Earlestowa Orchestral Society, a very pleasant evening being spent. The whole of the arrangements were carried out by the committee, with Mr Warburton as secretary. BARLESTOWN AND DISTR ICT P.S A. SOCIETY.

The Winter Session was opened on Sunday by what was termed an open Conference. There was only a mo&rate attendance, doubtless due to counter attractions at other places of worship. Mr Williamson pre.ided, and the chief business was the election of officials. Mr Dring, the town Missionary wa6s unable to be present owing to ill- ness, but he was unanimously elected Pre,ident, Mr. R. Thompqoa was appointed secretary. Ai r S Dring. principal Musical Director and Master A Dring, assistant. The whole of last Sessions Com- mittee were re-appointed. A discussion was opened upon the best way to improve on the work of last session, and Mr Jackson gave an address. Mr Williams a member of the staff of the Man- chester Mission,'‘, Mr Drink's locum tenens, also delivered an interesting address, and two musical selections were given by the choir. In the evening the service was conducted by Mr Williams when there was an encouraging congregation.

NORTHERN UNION.

LANCASHIRE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Warrington have hard Luck at Broughton

Warrington's fixture on Saturday was with Broughton Rangers at Wheater's Field, where they sustained defeat by a single point, the score being six points to 5. The result was very creditable to the home team who pulled the match out of the fire in the last few minutes after Warrington bad had most of the play. The match was the first at Broughton this year and the new turf got a good trial. It has metamor- phosised the appearance of Wheater's Field, which used to be far from attractive. The teams had the honour of the presence of Messrs J E Lawton (Liberal) and J Platt Higgins (Conservative) the two candidates for the division, one kicking off at the commencement and the other at half time. Vote-seeking on football fields seems to be getting very popular. Mr M'Cutcheon (Oldham) was referee, and the teams lined out as follows,the only absentee laAng J T Bate from the Warrington ranks. Warrington : Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Lawless, and Hockenhall, three-quarters; Burgess and D ickworth, half-backs; Taylor, Morrison, Mereweather Boardman, Scholtze, Eien, Anirews and Swift, for- wards.

Broughton Rangers: Harry, full back: Hogg. Wilson. Barratt and Williams. three-quarters; W James, and S James, half-backs ; Whitehead. Toon:11350a, Lazen- by. Clemiason, Davies, Reynolds, Hardisty, and Ruldock, forwards.

Warrington won the toss, hut there was no wind to give the advantage to either side. Mr. J. Platt Higgins set the ball in motion before 5,000 spectators, Lawless returning to centre, where play rested for some time. Warrington showed smartness in heeling out, and Burgess early broke away finely, and caused Harry to be tackled by Duckworth near the line. Broughton cleared, and a round of excellent pass- ing carried play to the Warrington end, Hogg making a strong effort to get in at the corner. Some exciting play followed at close quarters, Wilson also menacing the Warrington line. W James had a shot at goal, but kicked wide. 'Taylor removed the scene of operations with a lofty kick. Burgess made a fine dribble into the home half, where Morrison marked in good position. Fish took the kick but failed to land a goal. Hogg then had his kick charged down by Isherwood, Harry being lucky to save on the line. Warrington were now attacking hotly and a pass from Burgess to Isherwood resulted in the latter smartly dropping a goal when challenged. Before War- rington could be properly drawn back Fish made a fine effort and was knocked over the corner flag. Warring- ton had the best of the game, Burgess making several fine rues which would have resulted in points had he passed the ball sooner. Before the interval Fish dropped at goal but the ball passed under the bar. Half- time, Warrington 1 goal ; Rangers nil. Mr. J. E. Lawton started for Warrington, and his kick was not of much service.for Broughton immediately assumed the aggressive, Hallam making a plucky save. Burgess again dribbled finely, and Harry was bowled in touch.B James responded for the borne team,making a clever break.a way but Isherwood'grasse i him ere he got dangerous. A short kick by Barratt saw Hallam tackled near the line. A determined struggle ensued, the Ran- gers getting p/R3036i30 and Wi,sen being held close in. W. James threw across to fine'. who was clear, and dashed over at the corner, no goal resulting. Warring- ton roused up again at this and soon had the Rangers pennei on the line. Harry was teaed and failed to hold the ball for the first time, Mereweather dribbling over and scoring a try for Warrington- Fish failed at goal. It seemed long odds on Warrinkten now, but in the last ten minutes the home team played with great dash' and forced Warrington back. The same tactics as before resulted in Wilson rounding the Wa.rington backs and scoring a fine try.which won th match. There was no goal from W James' kick. Tneugh Warrington tried hard they could not score, Fish being held when he appeared to have a chance. Pnal : Broughton Rangers, two tries (6 points) ; War- rington 1 goal 1 try (5 points). 'nese reams met at Wildersp aol on Maturday before about 2.000 spectators. The Liberal candidate, Mr Arthur R. Crosfield, kicked off, and quick passing Raw Warrington aggressive. NORTH WICH DIVISION OF CHESHIRE. GENERAL ELECTION, 1900. SIR JOHN BRUNNER'S SIXTH ADDRESS TO THE ELECTORS. T O THE ELKCTORS OF THE NORTHWUCH DIVISION OF CHESHIRE. GENTLEMEN, Four times already you have done me the honour to elect me your representative in the House of Commons, and I respectfully solicit at your hands tbe honour, so highly prized by me, of representing you in a fifth Parliament. During the many years I have held this high office you have done me many a well-remembered act of kind ness. and I have rendered to you whole-hearted service with respect to affairs both national and local. By the advice of the Government, the General Election is to be held in the month of October. within four weeks of the date at which a new regieter comes Into force in Scotland, and within thiee months in England. Ireland and Wales. The Election is. therefore, to be held at a time when, through deaths and removals, the smallest possible number of Her Majesty's subjects in the United Kingdom will be entitled to a vote. Includ- ing our soldiers, now serving us with such splendid valour in South Africa. at least one in live is deprived of his constitutional right to pass judgment upon the acts of the Government. out of the number who would have enjoyed that right had the Election been post- Toned until January. That is to say, the Government thus disfranchises something like ten times as many as all the adult British males who worked in the Transvaal added together. By January, as I am happy to believe, the large majority of our soldiers will have returned home, and assuredly no others are so well entitled to be heard as they. They will have much to say about the management of the a ar. Under these circumstances, and especially looking to the fact that no General Election has been held in the autumn for nearly a hundred years. except upon a specially piepared new register, I consider the demand of the Government for an immediate appeal to the people to be no better than a solemn sham; and I conflaently ask you to condemn it accordingly. Mr. Chamberlain saes in his Address to the Electors of West Birmingham, that we are now asked to say whether or not the war was just and inevitahle, and that their opponents accuse the Government of deliberately pro- voking a war for which they made no preparation.l know of no Liberal in a responsible position who has thus accused the Government. But passing that by, I declare my opinion on the contrary to be that the Government never intented to go to war, an.' that they themselves. mislead by the assurances of Mr. Cecil Rhodes and his friends, had no idea that a war was in- evitable ; for how otherwise are we to account for the notorious fact that they were so ill pre- pared for it, in spite of having spent, last year, no less than twelve millions of money noon our Army and Navy more than the late Liberal Government spent in their last year of office. I have never said, and do not say now, that the war was unjust, for the reason that. it was we who were first attacked. We were bound to fight and to win. These are my answers to Mr. Chamberlain. At the same time I blame the Government for haying knowingly allowed the Transvaal for three years to con- tinue, unhindered, to accumulate enormous armaments— armaments which could only have been wanted for use against us ; and I consider qr. Balfour's plea that " our hands were tied by the Raid" to be an utterly inadequate excuse. This lack of moral courage on the part of the Government has cost us millions on millions of hard- earned money, and has brought bitter grief into thousands of humble homes. I blame the Government fer having, at the outset of the war, refused Australia's offer of mounted troops, and for having replied" Unmounted men preferred." This was no trivial and pardonable slip, but a huge and terrible blunder, which, coupled with the Inexcusable delay in sending out cavalry from home, resulted in disaster upon disaster to our troops during the sad months of last autumn and winter. a blunder for which alone this Government deserves t o be driven from office. Having myself, like so many of us, near relatives serving in South Africa, I bitterly re- sent those disasters, and I hold the Government, and not our soldiers, responsible for them. The settlement proposed with regard to the Transvaal and the Orange River Colony by Lord Salisbury, Mr. Balfour, and Mr. Chamberlain, is agreed to by Lord Rosebery, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, Sir William Harcourt. and Mr. Morley ; so that, epon this question, which the Tory Party are endeavouring to make the test question at this Election, there is absolutely no difference between the two great parties in the State. With this proposal so made and so agree I to. I am in complete accord. As heretofore, I appeal to you as a thorough going Liberal, steadily holding to the generoos side in pol Rice. I desire a Radical reform in our antiquated Land Laws, convinced long years ago by Richard Cobden that the party which brings about free trade in letel will do even more for our country than that which secured free trade In corn. I desire that the people should heye the pow 'r now vested in the magistrates with regard tot he ex:stenee of public houses in their immediate neighhaerheo I. Those who use the public houses, and those wt, a stiffer from the public houses, have a clear moral rieht to this power. No man should be a licensed victualler who 411 not sell victuals; and no victualler should be tied ta a brewer. I desire to amend our iniquitous system of local rating by securing that all rateable property shall pay according to its real aqual market value. All attempts to deal with tne pressing question 'of ever Tow ia in towns, without this change in thejaw, will be a mockery. I desire that the people, now pay: ner at least f s fifths of the cost of our Elementary Schools, should la-ive the full control of those schools, lam cenvincee that with regard to religious differences they wo lid be thoroughly fair and considerate towards each other. I strongly desire the amendment of the Workmen's Compensatioa Act, so as to include all workmen, and to provide fair compensation in all elVii of aeceione, 00 at on which the present Act is rielcolonsly inadequate. Fair compensation has been paid for all accidents by my firm since January, 1831. need— twenty years. We have not waited for the Tweee to fulfil their hypocritical promise to grant Old Age Pensions. Our men are the steadiest set of workers in t,ei• kinedom--1 hey grow old in our emnley, end when thee reline we grant pensions In ell necessitous en: es, depending upon their length of service. My ~pponents continue to r.-‘B.,t the flui slander that we refuse to take on 'nen nye.* thirty years of age ; we lake on men over thirty years of rage nearly every week in the year. Generous minded M 3,1 will resent this method of attack. Ilhope to elevote the rest of my working life fel weer service; but if any voter Its the cmistitnencv honestly believer that my opponent has been, or is, or is likely to be. fa better friend to England n better friend to Ches- hire, or a better friend to you. then I have been, am, and intend to be, he is, as far as I em eoncorned. welcome to express that belief, and to tetra( if, at the ballot box. Your faithful servant. JOHN T. BRUNN ER. Winnineton Old Hall. 26th September, 1930.

THE CRISIS IN CiilNA has caused much alarm. A few years ago it would have affected the comfort of every home In lingland by increasing the cost of tea. To-day, however, with the delightfully fragrant and refreshing British-grown tea which is being shipped weekly to BROOKE BOND tt Cott LTD" from India and Ceylon, tea-drinkers may regard the crisis with equanimity. Thanks to British capital and industry, India and Ceylon have become the great tea-gardens of the world, and we are no longer dependent on the long•eyed race who once held the tea monopoly. Although the capital invested in British tea-productien amounts to over £14,000,000, there is no tea more highly prized than BROOKE, BOND'S. It is obtained from estates in the finest stage of cultivation, is the choicest pick of each season. and is always exquisite and delicious.
